He, assisted by the work of one citizen, captured the city by guile, and having killed all the males, he leveled it to the ground. He also captured another city, Thebez, at the first assault. The urban multitude fled with difficulty into a tower situated in the middle of the city. As Abimelech threatened its gates, a woman dropped a fragment of a millstone upon his head and killed him. The leadership of the Hebrews was entrusted to Jair, who happened to be from the tribe of Manasseh. He governed for twenty-two years and died. Afterwards, the Ammonites along with the Philistines plundered the country. And when the Ammonites were ravaging the Gileadite land, the local inhabitants chose Jephthah as leader. As he was about to engage with the enemies, he made a vow that, if he should be victorious, he would sacrifice whatever first met him on his return.
And having joined battle, he was victorious. As he was returning, his only daughter met him, and she was still a virgin. He lamented, but in order not to break his promise, he sacrificed the girl and offered her as a whole burnt offering. When those of the tribe of Ephraim were angry with him, because he had not taken them along for the war against the Ammonites, since he could not persuade them by making excuses, he slew many who fought against him; and having completed his leadership for eight years, he ended his life. Then Abdon, from the tribe of Ephraim, took over the leadership, and having judged Israel for eight years, he died. And after him, Abdon, from the tribe of Ephraim, proceeded to be proclaimed leader. And he died in old age, having reigned for eight years.
XXIV. Next, the Philistines held the Israelites in subjection and kept them as tributaries for forty years. At this time, Samson was born (the name is significant of strength) and began to harass the Philistines. The account of his entry into life happened as follows: Manoah, a man of the tribe of Dan, had a barren wife, and they both prayed to God that a child might be given to them. Once, when she was alone, a great and handsome youth appeared to the woman, announcing to her the birth of a child, and commanded her neither to drink wine nor any strong drink, and not to cut the child's hair. He said that the child would be a Nazirite to the Lord, that is to say, holy, and that through him Israel would be saved from the hand of the foreigners. She reported these things to her husband. And as he longed for Him who had appeared to the woman to appear to him as well, he prayed to God. Then the angel came again to the woman. But Manoah was away from her. The woman waited for him and entreated the presence of her husband. When Manoah came, he asked the angel what they should do regarding the child who was to be born. He said, "He shall not drink wine, nor strong drink, nor shall he eat anything unclean, and his hair shall not be shorn." Manoah asked the name of him who was speaking to him, but he, saying that it was wonderful, kept it silent. C Manoah brought a kid and sacrificed it, and offered loaves. He ordered him to place the meat and the loaves upon the rock. When they were placed, he touched them with his staff, and fire, ignited from there, consumed them; and through that flame the angel ascended into heaven, while Manoah and his wife looked on.
After this, the boy was born, and he was named Samson. He grew up, and the Spirit of the Lord went with him. Having seen a girl of the foreigners in the city of Timnah of the Philistines, he fell in love with the girl. Although his parents declined the marriage with the foreigner, he insisted on marrying her. As he was frequently going to the girl's parents, he encountered a lion. And having no weapon for defense, he tore the beast apart with his hands, and turning aside from the road, he cast it toward the thicket. And after some days, again journeying toward his betrothed, he found a swarm of bees gathered in the mouth of the lion. And having taken a honeycomb from there, he ate it, and brought some to his father and mother. And having gone to the foreigners, he proposed a riddle to the thirty youths feasting with him at the wedding; and he agreed that if they should find the solution within seven days, he would give a linen cloth and a garment to each of them; but if they should not be able to solve the riddle, a garment and a linen cloth were requested from each of them. When the youths had agreed, he put forth the question, saying: "Out of the eater
came forth meat, and from the strong came forth sweetness." They, not being able to understand the riddle, besought the girl to learn it from her husband and report it to them. Sampson at first resisted, though she entreated him to tell her the meaning of the riddle. But when she wept, he told her the whole thing. And she reported it to the youths. And on the seventh day they said to Sampson: "What is sweeter than honey? or what is stronger than a lion?" Sampson was then angered, and he gave to the youths what he had promised, but he renounced the marriage. And the father gave the young woman to another. Sampson, provoked by this, caught three hundred foxes, and tied burning torches to their tails, and let them loose into the fields of the Philistines, the summer being at hand. And thus he burned their standing grain and their vineyards. The Philistines, in retaliation for this, burned his former wife and her relatives in the fire. Sampson, having killed many of the foreigners, sat upon a rock; and they campaigned against the Israelites, saying: "If you wish to be innocent, deliver Sampson into our hands." And three thousand armed men went to the rock. Sampson, coming down, voluntarily surrendered himself to his tribesmen. And they bound him and delivered him to the Philistines. But he broke the bonds, and having snatched up the jawbone of an ass that was lying there, he killed a thousand of the foreigners with it, and put the rest to flight; whence that place was called "Jawbone" from that time. Then he thirsted so much that he was failing from thirst, and he wept, beseeching God. And God caused a spring of water to gush forth from the fissure which Sampson had made by throwing the jawbone against the ground. And he went away to Gaza. The Gazites lay in wait for him; and so that he might not escape them, they closed the city gates. But he, rising about midnight, took up the gates, and their thresholds and doorposts upon his shoulders, and carried them to the top of the mountain, and set them down there. Having fallen in love with a Philistine harlot named Dalida, he lived with her. The leaders of the foreigners promised much to the woman if she would learn from Sampson the cause of such strength and report it to them. She variously tempted the man, seeking to know whence he had such strength. But he, feigning other reasons at different times, spoke to the woman. And the woman, testing his words, found them all to be false. But as she pressed him and begged him more warmly, he revealed the secret to her: saying that by the command of God he had to grow his hair, for He had forbidden him to cut it; and that in this his strength resided. C The woman cut his hair while he was sleeping, and delivered him as a weakling to his enemies. They put out the man’s eyes, and he who had formerly been formidable was kept for their sport. In the course of time, his hair grew again, and his strength returned. When the Philistines were drinking, and their leaders and magnates were feasting together in a house supported by columns, Sampson was also brought by a boy to be mocked. A multitude of about three thousand men and women gathered by the house to witness the mockery of Sampson. As he was being insulted with jeers and struck with slaps, he ordered the boy who was guiding him to lead him to the columns: "So that, being weary," he said, "I may lean upon them and rest." Then, having dislodged the two columns, he brought down the building, by the ruin of which he himself and all who were in it were crushed. And such an end he had, having held judgment over the Israelite people for twenty years. D
XXV. Of the Judges, and of Ruth and Boaz, and of the High Priest Eli
XXV. No longer did Judges lead Israel, but the high priest Eli presided over them, during whose time a famine occurred. A certain man, Abimelech by name, from Bethlehem, emigrated with his wife and two sons to the land of Moab because of the famine. There he betrothed Moabitish wives to his sons, and within ten years, he died together with his sons. His widow, the wife Noemis, was left behind, along with both daughters-in-law of her sons, Orpha and Ruth. Noemis returned to her homeland, and of her daughters-in-law, Orpha, upon the advice of her mother-in-law, remained among the Moabites. But Ruth went with Noemis. And when they had already arrived in Bethlehem, Boaz, who was a kinsman of Abimelech, took them in. Being addressed by the citizens by her name, the woman said, "Call me not Noemis, but call me Mara (for Noemis signifies happiness, but Mara signifies pain and bitterness); for I went out full, but I have returned empty." At the time of the harvest, Ruth, according to the advice of her mother-in-law, went out to glean in the field of Boaz. He, having seen the maiden and learned who she was, permitted her to glean whatever she could and carry it away, and ordered the steward to provide her with a meal. And she, having also received grain from him, kept some for her mother-in-law. And she went home in the evening, carrying the barley which she had gathered, and reported to her how she had been treated by Boaz, and she went out again with the maidservants of Boaz. But Noemis devised a plan for Ruth to lie down with him, and she sent her daughter-in-law, having instructed her to lie down at the feet of the man as he slept on the threshing floor. And she did so. Boaz, feeling something about the middle of the night, asked who it was; and having learned, he was quiet for that time; but in the morning, having awakened Ruth, he ordered her to depart to her mother-in-law. "I myself," he said, "will question him who is of closer kin to you than I; and if he wishes to take you, you shall go to that man; but if he refuses, you shall lawfully be married to me." So the woman went away and declared everything to Noemis. And Boaz gathered together Ruth and the kinsman before the elders, and said to him: "Do you hold the inheritance of our kinsman Abimelech and his sons?" And upon his agreeing that he held it because of the relationship according to the law, Boaz said, "Then the laws must not be observed by half, but one must comply with them in all things; therefore, you must take as a wife the woman of Mallon, son of Abimelech, so that you may raise up seed to your kinsman." But he refused, yielding to him both the inheritance and the woman. Therefore, according to the law before the elders, Ruth unloosed the man’s sandal and spat in his face. And thus Boaz took her, and she bore him Obed (which name signifies "serving"). From Obed was born Jesse, and from him David, who reigned over the people of Israel and left the dominion to his sons, propagated to the twenty-first generation. But of Eli, who, as we have said, governed the people, there were two sons, men wicked and covered in every kind of crime, Ophnis and Phinees; and they were not moved in the least by their father’s admonitions. God, angered by their iniquity, signaled through Samuel, while he was still a boy, the calamities impending upon Eli himself, his children, and his house. Now Samuel was the son of the Levite Elcana, who had two wives, Anna and
Fhenanpam: of whom the latter had borne children to Elcana, but Anna had not. When her husband traveled up to Silo with his wives, and when the religious rites were completed and the time for the feast arrived, and the children of Phenenna sat nearby, Anna lamented her childlessness; and hastening into the tabernacle of the Lord, she prayed to God that He might give her children, vowing that she would consecrate her firstborn to God. While she was lingering in prayer, Eli, sitting before the tabernacle and thinking her to be drunk, ordered her to leave. But she said: "I have drunk neither wine, my lord, nor any other intoxicating thing, but I am pining away in the sorrow of my childlessness." Then the priest said: "Go, and may God grant you your petition." Having returned home with her husband, she conceived, and gave birth to Samuel, whom one might call "Deo-plaitos" (beseeded by God). When the boy was already somewhat grown, she brought him to Eli according to her vow, to be educated for God, letting his hair grow long, living his life in the house of God, and using water for his drink. Being twelve years old, he began to prophesy. And at a certain time, as he was sleeping, he was called by name by God. He went to the high priest as if he had been called by him; and this happened three times. Then Eli, understanding that the calling was from God, ordered Samuel, if he should be called again, to say: "Speak, Lord, for thy servant hears." When the boy had done so, He who had called said to him that a great disaster was impending over the Israelites, that the sons of Eli would die together, and that the priesthood would pass over to the house of Eleazar. Although Samuel was unwilling to reveal these words to the priest, yet being compelled by oaths, he recounted everything just as he had heard.
XXVI. Then the Philistines made war upon the Israelites and came out superior in the battle. The people demanded that the Ark be brought to their aid. When it was sent there with the sons of Eli, the battle was renewed, the Hebrews were routed, and about thirty thousand fell, among whom were the sons of the high priest; and the Ark also fell into the hands of the enemies. Eli the high priest, who was sitting upon a high chair, upon learning of the slaughter of the army, the killing of his sons, and the capture of the Ark, fell from his chair out of grief and died, being ninety years old, having ruled the people for forty years. The foreigners brought the Ark into the city of Azotus and consecrated it to Dagon (for so they called their god). In the morning, having entered into the temple of Dagon, they found the statue cast down upon the ground before the Ark, and having lifted it up, they returned it to its pedestal. But as they found this happening more often, and as many times as they set up the idol, they found it cast down in the posture of one worshipping the Ark, they were amazed and perplexed. After this, a plague invaded the city and territory of the Azotians; for both men were afflicted about their seats (Josephus says it was dysentery), and many citizens died, and mice sprang from the earth to devastate all the fields. Finally, late in time, they realized that the Ark was the cause of their evils.
to the Azotians, and they sent it to Ascalon, and those people suffered the same. And thus the Ark passed through five cities of the Philistines, and it brought harm to them all. The magistrates of what are called the five cities then gathered—of Geth, Accaron, Ascalon, Gaza, and Azotus. And some recommended one thing regarding the Ark, and others another. At last it seemed best to all to make five golden seats and as many golden mice and place them upon the Ark, and to construct a new chariot, and to yoke to the chariot cows that had first given birth, but to keep their calves, and having placed the Ark upon the chariot, to take the cows away and leave them at a crossroads, so that in the direction they set out, it might be clear where they were to go. And if they were to go straight toward the Israelites, they were to deem that the evils were brought upon them on account of the Ark, and not to obstruct their journey to those people. But if they were to turn elsewhere, the cows and the chariot and what was upon it were to be turned back. Having thus carried out the things that were decided, they let the cows go, following from afar themselves so that they might see what would come to pass. But the cows went straight toward the Hebrews, and did not stop until they arrived at a large plain at Bethsames. When the men of that village saw the Ark, they rushed toward it; and having taken it down from the chariot, they sacrificed the cows, and with the wood from which the chariot was constructed, they burned them as a whole burnt offering. The Philistines, having seen this, returned home. But God, being angry at those who had touched the Ark, since they were profane, brought death upon them. The rest informed the common assembly of the Hebrews about the Ark; they took it and brought it to Cariathiarim, and placed it in the house of Aminadab (this man was of the Levitical tribe). And it remained there twenty years, though it had been with the aliens for four months. C Now Samuel the prophet, already having a great name and enjoying great authority, gathered the people and spoke to them about defending their liberty, and persuaded them to lay claim to it. When the Philistines learned of this, they came against the Hebrews unexpectedly. And they terrified them, so that they ran to Samuel, saying that their salvation was despaired of, unless he himself should entreat divine aid for them. And he bade them be of good courage, and assured them that God would defend them; and having sacrificed a lamb, he prayed to God. And not yet had the flame consumed the sacrifice of the holy rite, when the enemy attacked the Hebrews, thinking they could more easily overcome them as they were unarmed. But as the earth was shaken by God, and thunders were unleashed, and lightnings were flashed forth, the enemy fell into a cowardice and turned to flight. Samuel and the multitude pursued them and slew many, and after this defeat they no longer campaigned against the Israelites, but Samuel also recovered the land which they had taken from the Hebrews. Thus he judged the people and ruled them. D
XXVII.
Now already an old man, he distributed the governance of the people and the administration of justice among his sons; the elder was named Joel, and the younger, Abia. They did not walk in the ways of their father, but, turning aside, they traded justice for bribes.
The people, taking this ill, gathered to Samuel and said, "Since you are not able, by reason of age, to preside over us, appoint for us a king who shall lead the nation and battle against our enemies." When Samuel was distressed at these words, God said to him, "It is not you whom the people have rejected, but me. Listen to them, therefore, and I will reveal to you whom you shall anoint as king over them." Having gathered the Hebrews, Samuel agreed to appoint a king for them. He prophesied to them, however, that they would repent and would eventually seek from God to be rid of their kings. But they pressed him, seeking a king, and begged him not to be concerned about the future. The Prophet said, "Depart now, and when I have learned whom God gives us as king, I will send for you." Now there was a certain Benjamite (Cis was the man's name) who had lost his donkeys and sent his son, who was large and handsome—he was called Saul—to search for the donkeys, along with one of his servants. Having gone around and searched, and not finding them, he arrived at Armathaim to Samuel, intending to inquire about the donkeys. God therefore revealed to Samuel that this was the one he wished to rule over the people of the Hebrews. When Samuel was asked about the donkeys, he told him they were safe. And having hosted Saul, he sent him off in the morning. And walking with him outside the city, and having taken the young man aside, he poured oil upon his head. And having kissed him, he said, "The Lord has anointed you as ruler over His people Israel. And let this be a sign of the anointing: that upon meeting you by the tomb of Rachel, two men will congratulate you on the finding of the donkeys." He also told him other signs of his truthfulness, and that he would prophesy at Gabatha, having come among the prophets there. And he commanded him to come to Galgala when sent for by him. He then assembled the people and ordered the tribes to cast lots so that it might be revealed from which one the king would be. The lot fell upon the tribe of Benjamin, then upon the clan of Battari. When the lot was cast man by man of the clan, it fell upon Saul. As he could not be found when sought, Samuel was informed by God where he was hidden; and being brought forward, he set him in the midst. And he was of tall stature, and stood head and shoulders above all. And the prophet said to the multitude, "This is he whom the Lord has anointed as your king, to whom there is no likeness among you all." The people cheered and said, "Long live the king!" Thus, once Saul had become king, many respected him as king and honored him as was fitting, but many also despised and mocked him. After a month, Naas, king of the Ammanites, besieged Jaben of Galaad, saying he would spare the men on no other condition than that each would let him gouge out his right eye. They sought a truce of seven days; having obtained this, they sent messengers into Galaad. Upon hearing the words of the Ammanite, the people mourned, but Saul, stirred by a great rage, sent two oxen cut into pieces throughout all the borders of the Israelites, threatening that he would treat in the same manner those who did not come to beat back the Ammanites. A gathering of the people having taken place, and a count being made, some seven hundred thousand men were found; and Saul, filled with the divine spirit, predicted that the inhabitants of Jaben would be saved on the next day. And having marched all night with the army, about the first watch he arrived at the Ammanites.
are taught to Daniel through these things; for he was emboldened by the kings before him—those, clearly, from Seleucus—and having mastered Egypt and other lands, he campaigned against the Jews, and captured the city of Jerusalem, but also the entire nation. And he plundered the temple and defiled it, by sacrificing swine in it, and by erecting an altar to Zeus; he compelled the whole nation to Hellenize, forbidding them to circumcise their males. For his madness against God, “it was magnified up to the power of heaven,” implies this. And as for, “They fell from the power and from the stars to the ground, and he trampled upon them,” he signifies those of the Jewish people who apostatized because of the necessity brought upon them by him, those who fell away from the legal and divine way of life, yielding to the tyrant and being trampled by him. They were called “stars” because of the purity and brilliance of their piety, or because God had promised Abraham that his seed would multiply like the stars of heaven. “And it was set up until the ruler of the power.” For he even provoked the ruler of the powers above, God Himself. “And the sacrifice was disturbed by a corpse, and it was brought about, and it was smelled, and sin was given upon the sacrifice, and justice was thrown to the ground.” For by sacrificing forbidden things, he disturbed the sacrifice—instead of that, he confused it, he defiled it. By “corpses,” he means the swine, and by “sin upon the sacrifice,” the lawlessness concerning it. And by the words, “Justice was thrown to the ground,” the annulment of the divine laws is shown, and the unjust murders of those who did not consent to commit impiety. But even while doing these things, and acting so impiously, Antiochus, he says, was prosperous. Upon these things, Daniel adds: “And one holy one said to Phelmuni, ‘How long will the vision last, and the sacrifice that was taken away, and the sin of desolation that was given?’ And he said to him, ‘Until evening and morning, two thousand three hundred days, and the holy place shall be cleansed.’” Because the prophet was anxious over the things occurring, angels were sent to him, informing him that the difficult times would have an end, and teaching him the time of their passing. And one asked the other. For “Phelmuni,” when rendered into Greek, signifies a certain one. He asked not as one ignorant, but so that Daniel might learn. But he who was asked replied: “Two thousand three hundred days until evening and morning, and the holy place shall be cleansed.” By “evening,” he called the beginning of the calamities, or the whole time of them—for troubles are likened to night and darkness—and by “morning,” the peaceful state after the disasters. Therefore, this is what he signifies by the words he says, that from the beginning of the evils until their end, so much time will pass. And the days, when counted as years, produce six months and as many years, according to the calculation of the Hebrews. For for that long the calamity brought upon the Jews by Antiochus prevailed. These things were revealed to the prophet, who was seeking understanding, by the archangel Gabriel. IX. NINTH Then, again, because of the captivity of his fellow countrymen, under Darius the ruler of the Medes—who was also called Cyaxares and Assuerus, the son of Astyages—as the prophet was lamenting and praying to God, a man touched him, whose name was Gabriel. He touched him about the time of the evening sacrifice, whether the time of the evening worship is meant, or the duration of time during which the evening sacrifice is performed. And he said to Daniel: “I have now come forth to give you understanding, and to announce to you, for you are a man of desires. Therefore, pay attention to what is said, and understand the vision;” that is, listen attentively. For the words are obscure,
he attacked, and killed about twenty. He struck such fear into the rest that, thrown into confusion and fleeing, they destroyed one another. Saul, seeing the camp of the enemy in disorder, and noticing that his son together with his armor-bearer was missing, he himself also made an assault upon the bewildered adversaries; and those who before had been hiding out of fear, taking heart, joined themselves to the king, and while pursuing the enemies, they destroyed them. Saul then cursed his men if anyone should taste food before nightfall, with the slaughter of the enemies interrupted. During the pursuit, Jonathan, ignorant of his father’s curse, when he came into a wood where there was a beehive, ate of an honeycomb; for he had not heard of his father’s curse. Desiring to attack the camp of the adversaries, Saul asked God through the high priest whether He would grant victory; but when God did not reveal anything, he sought the author of the divine offense; and, "I swear," he said, "by God Himself, that I will kill the one who has sinned, even if it be my son Jonathan." Lots were cast, and Jonathan was designated, who, being asked what he had done wrong, said that he was not conscious of any sin, except that the day before, ignorant of his father’s curse, he had tasted of the honeycomb in the course of the pursuit. His father, having heard this, swore that he would kill him. The people, on the contrary, swore that they would not allow the author of the victory to be put to death; and having rescued him, they appeased God with prayers. Saul, having killed about sixty thousand of the Philistines, returned; and having subdued the Ammonites, Moabites, Philistines, Idumeans, and Amalekites, and the king of Soba, he delivered the Israelites from the hand of the foreigners.
XXIX. Saul had three sons, Jonathan, Jesus, and Melchisus; and two daughters, Meroba and Melchol. He had made Abner, the son of his kinsman Ner, commander-in-chief. Samuel had said to Saul that God commanded him to strike Amalec, and, having gained victory, to spare no one—not women, not infants, not beasts of burden, not flocks—but to blot out their name from the earth. Therefore, Saul, having engaged with the Amalekites, conquered the enemies and killed them all. But Agag, the king of that land, he did not kill, having been brought into his power, but out of admiration for his form and stature, he spared him; and he also kept the herds, the flocks, and other choice things, forgetful of the divine command. Then Samuel, instructed by God, who said that He repented [(49)] that He had made Saul king, met the king. He said that he thanked God, by whose gift he had vanquished the enemies and had done all that was commanded. But the prophet said: "I hear the voice of beasts of burden and other flocks. Whence are these?" Saul answered that the people had saved them for a sacrifice to God, but that all the Amalekites had been destroyed, except for their king. To these words Samuel said: "Since you have not obeyed the commands of God, know that you shall lose the kingdom." But he, having confessed that he had sinned, begged the Prophet that through sacrifices he might reconcile God to himself.
the land of Egypt, and lay them waste and bring them to desolation. Having therefore arrayed himself against Arphaxad the Mede, and having mastered him by force, and having taken his other cities, as well as Ecbatana itself, where the kingdom of Arphaxad was—and having captured and killed him—he summoned Holofernes, the commander-in-chief of his own forces, and instructed him to fulfill the oath he had sworn, and to campaign with a heavy force against those who had not wished to ally with him in his invasion against the Medes. He ordered him to preserve those who submitted themselves to him and to leave their cities unravaged, but to show no mercy to those who disobeyed, but rather to put the people to the sword and deliver their cities to plunder and destruction. Thus did Nabuchodonosor command his arch-satrap. Holofernes, having set out and having come against the nations against whom he was sent, disposed of the others according to his instructions. But as he was about to campaign against the Israelites and take up arms against them (for they did not approach him nor submit), he asked who these people were and in whom they trusted that they resisted. And Achior, leader of the children of Ammon, told him of their genealogy, and how they had settled in those cities from the beginning, and [of] the power of their God. Then he added: "Now, therefore, it must be considered, and if they are sinning against their God, we shall go up and make war upon them; but if not this, let my Lord pass them by, lest their God shield them and we be defeated." When Achior had said these things, Holofernes, seized with anger, commanded those who stood by to bind Achior and take him away and deliver him to the children of Israel, that he might be punished, having been captured along with them. And those who seized the man led him away to Bethulia. And the men of the city cast stones and arrows at those approaching. And they threw the bound Achior beneath the hillside and returned. The men of the city, having gone down, released Achior and brought him into their own city, and inquired what had happened, and he recounted everything to them. And they called upon God for defense. Upon the next day, Holofernes led his army against Bethulia, being one hundred and seventy thousand infantry, and twelve thousand cavalry, besides the baggage-train, and he encamped beside it. Then he decided not to fight, but to seize the waters whence they drew, so that, failing by thirst, they might either perish or deliver the city to him. And he stationed a part of his army as guards at the waters. And for thirty-four days, while the enemy sat against them, the water failed them; and the people were gathered to the rulers, and they demanded that he deliver the city to the enemies, and themselves, that they might serve them and live, or, having been slaughtered, might be freed from their pains. And Ozias, the leading man of the city, said: "Stay, brothers, for five days; and if help comes to us from God, it will be well; but if not, I will do according to your counsel." XII. Now there was in that city a woman named Judith; and the woman was a widow, and she was temperate, and prudent, and beautiful in appearance. Having heard what had been decreed by the people and the rulers, she sent for Ozias and the other rulers of the city. And she reproached them for tempting God, because they said they would deliver the city to the enemies after five days, if within them no help should come to them from God. And while they conceded that she spoke well, they nevertheless set against her the thirst and the violence of the people. Then she, through that night, at the city gates...
First, therefore, Herod examined with tortures as many as he thought were faithful to Alexander, to see if they knew of anything daring committed against him; but they died having nothing to say. One, however, not bearing the tortures, said that Alexander used to speak, while being praised for the size of his body and his marksmanship with the bow and other things, that if any good quality were given him by nature or acquired through training, it turned into a misfortune; for his father was vexed at his good qualities. He added further that he had even plotted with Aristobulus, while out hunting, to kill his father and flee to Rome, intending to lay claim to the kingdom. Letters of Alexander were also found addressed to his brother saying that the father was not acting justly in preferring Antipater. On these charges, he arrested and imprisoned Alexander. He was eager, however, to obtain a greater proof against his son, so that he would not seem to have imprisoned him rashly. He therefore tortured and put to death many of the men in authority, though they said nothing of the sort he imagined. But when one of the younger men was subjected to these pressures, he said that Alexander used to write to friends in Rome, asking to be called by Caesar, intending to inform him that Mithridates, the king of the Parthians, had made a treaty of friendship with his father against the Romans; and that he even had a poison prepared in Ascalon. Herod considered these things a consolation for his own rashness. The poison, however, was immediately searched for but not found. Because of the excess of these evils, Alexander, being aggressively disposed and wishing to avenge himself on his enemies so that they would perish along with him, sent a letter to his father saying that there was no need for torture; for the plot had been contemplated, and Pheroras and those of his friends most faithful to him were participants in it; and that Salome had even come into him by night and had intercourse with him against his will; furthermore, that all had come to the same conclusion, so that once he was removed from the middle, they might be rid of the expectation of being perpetually ruined. Archelaus, the king of the Cappadocians, having learned these things, and being anxious for both his daughter and his son-in-law, arrived, and skillfully set about correcting the things that caused grief, condemning the young man, yet calling Herod reasonable, and saying that he would dissolve the marriage. As Archelaus said such things, Herod began to yield in his severity and was moved to grief and tears, and begged him not to dissolve the marriage, but to relax his anger regarding the errors the young man had committed. Archelaus, seeing that Herod was already softened, shifted the accusations onto others, and more specifically onto Pheroras. Pheroras turned to Archelaus, so that he might be a reconciler for him with Herod. And he advised him that it would be better to approach his brother through himself and to implore him, admitting himself the cause of all things; for he would thus soften the hardness of his anger; and he also promised to be present and help. Pheroras was persuaded. And Alexander was absolved of the charges, and Archelaus reconciled Pheroras to Herod. After a time, however, affairs concerning the children and the house went much worse for Herod. For Eurycles from Lacedaemon, a man not insignificant among those there, but most wicked in his character, having visited Herod, gave him gifts, and having received more in return, became one of the king’s greatest friends. He had his lodging among those of Antipater; but he also approached Alexander, claiming to be acquainted with Archelaus as well, whence he also feigned to honor Glaphyra. To this man, having become familiar, Alexander dramatized his own affairs, and instructed him about his mother, saying that it seemed unbearable. And while he, being in pain, had spoken such words to Eurycles, he reported everything to Antipater. Antipater greeted him with gifts and asked him to relate these words to his father. And he so disposed the king as to make his hatred irreconcilable. And while he departed having received payment, Herod was so enraged against his children that he no longer needed slanders, but he himself scrutinized their affairs and observed every single thing. Two of the king's bodyguards, having been dismissed because of his anger, began riding with those around Alexander and receiving gifts from him. Herod, therefore, suspecting them, immediately tortured them. At first they held out, but then they reported that Alexander was persuading them to kill Herod as he went hunting. The garrison commander of Alexandreion was also examined, as having promised to receive the youths in the fortress. And he admitted nothing, but his son testified that these things had happened, and delivered letters regarding these matters, as one might guess, in Alexander’s own hand. Herod, therefore, was no longer doubtful about the plot of the children, while Alexander insisted that the short letter had been forged through the machinations of Antipater. At that time, therefore, the youths were held in custody, and they were held in disrepute and fear as condemned men. Aristobulus, thinking that his mother-in-law Salome shared his pain and hated the one who was persuaded of such things, said: "These things foretell danger also for you, as you have been slandered, in the hope of a marriage with Syllaeus." That woman immediately brought these words to her brother. He ordered both his sons to be bound and separated from each other, and for each to write down as much as they had contemplated against their father. They write that they had neither intended nor conspired in any plot against their father, but had contemplated a flight, and this out of necessity, their life being suspect and terrifying to them. At that time, therefore, an envoy coming from Archelaus arrived; he brought out Alexander in chains, and inquired about the flight in the presence of the envoy—where and how they had decided to depart. He said that he had intended to send him from there to Rome having made an agreement, but that he had considered nothing else improper or troublesome against his father.
Now that Antipater had made away with his brothers, the attainment of the kingdom was more laborious for him, as hatred toward him had grown in the nation, and especially in the military. For a time, however, he governed alongside his father and was trusted by him. Herod sent Alexander's wife back to her father, but he reared the children of the deceased with great care. Alexander had two sons by Glaphyra, and Aristobulus had three sons and two daughters by Berenice. To the king there was also born a son, Herod, by the daughter of the high priest. For there lived with the king nine wives, it being a custom to cohabit with several at once. Among them were the mother of Antipater and the daughter of the high priest, from whom, as has been said, a son of the same name as the king was born to him. He also had one wife who was the daughter of his brother, and a cousin along with her—that is to say, a first cousin; for the ancients called first cousins *anepsiai*, as can be found in many places, both in Plutarch and in the legal books. There was among Herod's wives one from the nation of the Samaritans, whose children were Antipas, Archelaus, and their daughter Olympias. Cleopatra of Jerusalem was also numbered among his wives; her children were Herod and Philip. Pallas was also among his consorts, who had borne him a son named Phasael. And in addition to the others, Phaedra and Elpis lived with him. Herod also had two daughters by Mariamne, and by Phaedra and Elpis daughters were born to him, Roxane and Salome. This, then, was the family of Herod; but matters looked only toward Antipater. And he was fearful to all, not so much by his power as by the wickedness of his character; and especially Pheroras courted him and was similarly courted in return. Salome was opposed to them, behaving toward them in a manner contrary to the meaning of her own name; for while the one (Salome) signified peace and indicated that the one so named should be peaceful, she was nonetheless exceedingly warlike, and incited others to wars, factions, and the alienation of kin. She, observing the intimacy between Antipater and Pheroras, told Herod that their goodwill was to his own detriment. Since they knew of Salome's calumny, they practiced hatred and insults against each other in public, while secretly they maintained their harmony toward one another. But they did not escape the notice of Salome, who scrutinized everything and reported to the king their secret meetings, banquets, and shadow councils. He, too, understood most of these things. The Pharisees, who held sway over the women’s quarters—men who prided themselves on their precise knowledge of the ancestral law and who opposed the king—they, while everyone else had sworn to be loyal to Caesar and King Herod, refused, being over six thousand in number. Favoring the wife of Pheroras, they predicted to Herod that the end of his reign had been decreed by God, and that it would be distributed to Pheroras and those from him. Nor did these things escape Salome’s malice, and they were immediately reported to the king. Those of the Pharisees who were convicted on these charges were put to death, as was the eunuch Bagoas, and a certain Carus, who was distinguished by the beauty of his body and was a favorite of Herod, as well as others among the king's company, as conspirators. Then the king, having convened a council, accused the wife of Pheroras and enumerated many charges, and finally, turning his speech toward Pheroras, he said that it was necessary for him to have sent the woman away of his own accord even before this for such reasons. "Now, at any rate, if you value my kinship, desist from living with her." He replied that he would choose to die rather than be deprived of his wife while living. From that point, Herod forbade Antipater and his mother to associate with Pheroras or to meet with his wives. They agreed to this, but they met with one another and caroused whenever there was an opportunity. Antipater, suspecting his father’s anger, wrote to his friends in Rome to send a letter to Herod requesting that he send Antipater to Caesar. Antipater was therefore sent to Rome with gifts, carrying also a will of Herod that gave the kingdom to him, but if he should happen to die first, it was to pass to Herod, who was born of the daughter of the high priest. Herod ordered Pheroras, if he would not release his wife, to go away. And he departed for his tetrarchy, swearing not to come back before he learned that his brother had died. So much so that, when the king fell ill and he was summoned, he did not obey, in order not to break his oath. Herod, however, later came to Pheroras of his own accord when he fell ill, and honored him when he died. The death of Pheroras was the beginning of evils for Antipater, for his uncle was avenging him for his fratricide. Two of Pheroras’s own freedmen demanded that Herod not leave his brother’s death unavenged, as he had been destroyed by poison. These claims, seeming credible, stirred the king to an investigation, and women, both bond and free, were tortured. The others endured, keeping silent, but one woman said nothing else but called upon God for Antipater’s mother to fall into such necessities as the cause of these evils. This moved Herod to a greater search; and the women, subjected to even fiercer tortures, made disclosures about the revels, the secret meetings, and the words Antipater had spoken to the wives of Pheroras, his hatred for his father, and his lamentations to his mother that his father’s life was being prolonged for so long that he would not even enjoy the pleasures of the kingdom, if it ever came to him, on account of the old age already pressing upon him; and the women also said many other things attacking the father. Having already convicted Antipater based on these things, the king took away from his mother, Doris, all the finery around her, worth many talents, and sent her away.
I have written to your father the letter you wanted, and after making a copy of the one sent to my mistress, as if sent by Salome, I have dispatched it; I know that when she comes, she will punish Salome. The letter to Herod was as follows: "As I always take care that nothing that happens against you escapes your notice, I found a letter from Salome to my mistress against you, and I have sent you a copy of it, at personal risk to myself, but for your benefit." This Acme was a Jewess by birth, a slave to Julia, the wife of Caesar, and she did these things having been bought with large sums of money by Antipater. Herod immediately set out to slay Antipater as a goad to great affairs, and Salome incited him, beating her breast and demanding that she be put to death if he found any credible evidence against her. Restraining himself, he sent for his son, ordering him to say nothing and feel no suspicion. He, however, laid the responsibility for everything upon Antiphilus. Herod again sent letters to Caesar accusing Antipater, revealing as well how much Acme had been found to have conspired in the wickedness; he also sent copies of her letters. In the meantime, the king fell into sickness and wrote his will, giving the kingdom to the youngest of his sons, out of hatred for Archelaus and Philip, stemming from Antipater’s calumnies. Having despaired of living any longer, being around his seventieth year, he had become savage; the cause was that he thought he was being held in contempt and that everyone rejoiced at his misfortunes. He had been driven to this by the following events: Many things had been done by Herod against the law, one of which was the golden eagle set up above the great gate of the temple. The law forbids the setting up of images or the practice of depicting animals. Judas and Matthias, most learned of the Jews and popular with the people for their instruction of the youth, ordered this eagle to be torn down. For even if a danger should arise to anyone because of this, they said, that person is blessed for dying on behalf of the laws. While they were inciting the youth with such words, word went around that the king had died. And immediately, in the middle of the day, they pulled down the eagle and hacked it to pieces with axes. Therefore, many of the young men are arrested, as well as Judas and Matthias, the instigators of the daring act and the encouragers of those who carried it out, and they were brought before Herod. He asked if they had dared to take down his dedication. And the men said, "It has been done; and one should not wonder if we prefer the laws which Moses has left us, having been instructed by God, over your decrees." He sent the prisoners to Jericho, and having called those in authority among the Jews, he recalled, while reclining on a small couch, the favors he had conferred upon them and the building of the temple, and he cried out that even while he was still alive they were insulting him by tearing down his dedications, as if to seem to be insulting him, but in truth to be committing sacrilege. Fearing his cruelty, they declared that those who had dared this were worthy of punishment. The king treated the others more mildly, but took the honor of the high priesthood away from Matthias and installed another to perform the sacred rites, Joazar, the brother of his own wife; but he burned Matthias, the one who had stirred up the sedition, and some of the seditious men alive. However, the disease fastened more severely upon him, as he paid the penalty for his lawless deeds. A slow fire consumed his inner parts, there were ulcerations of the bowels, especially suffering of the colon, and fluid and clear phlegm around his feet, as well as a wretched state of the abdomen and putrefaction of the genitals which bred worms; and in addition to these, there was difficulty in breathing, unpleasant both in its odor and in the persistent asthma, and spasms occurred in every one of his limbs. Yet, even in such a wretched state, he was still in hope of life, not refusing to make use of those who promised healing. Having crossed the Jordan, he made use of the hot baths at Callirrhoe, which are potable along with their other medicinal virtue. There, having been lowered by his physicians into a tub of oil, he appeared to expire. Upon reviving, he was carried off to Jericho, where black bile overcame him above all else. Near death, he devised an unholy act. For by his command, as the most honorable men from every part of the nation had arrived there, he shut them all up in the hippodrome and instructed his sister Salome and her husband Alexas that, whenever he should breathe his last, they were to kill all those who were locked up, saying, "So that the peoples may not rejoice at my death, nor applaud, but that with each mourning their own people, I too may seem to be mourned, and my departure may be honored by many tears." And he enjoined these things weeping and beseeching, and they promised they would not transgress the command. In the midst of this, letters sent from Rome arrived, revealing that Acme had been put to death and leaving the matter regarding Antipater to the judgment of his father. Upon hearing these things, Herod was pleased and felt some slight relief. But as his pains grew great, he refrained from food, and having asked for an apple and a knife, he meant to kill himself. And he would have carried out his intention, had not someone who had foreseen it held his right hand. And as he cried out loudly, there was wailing and a great tumult, as though the king had passed away. And Antipater, as though the king were already dead, took heart and asked the jailer to release him, promising great rewards.
umphantly they should make their way toward them. For Judas, the son of the arch-robber Hezekiah, who had been captured by Herod with great effort, gathered a multitude, ran to the royal palace near Sepphoris in Galilee, and by seizing the arms there, he armed those around him and became a terror to everyone. But also Simon, a slave of King Herod, a man of handsome appearance and excelling in the height and strength of his body, being stirred up by the confusion of the times, dared to put on a diadem. And a multitude having gathered around him, he was also proclaimed king himself and set fire to the royal palace in Jericho, as well as others of the royal houses, after plundering what was in them. But Gratus joined battle with him, destroyed the greater part of the multitude with him, and having caught hold of Simon’s head, he cut it off. And other insurgents also arose during that time, harming both the Romans and the royalists. Varus, having learned in Syria of the things concerning Sabinus, hastened to aid those besieged in Judea. When all his force was assembled at Ptolemais, having handed over a certain part of it to his son, he sent him to make war on the Galileans; the latter, having collided with them, prevailed. Varus, advancing with the rest of the army to Jerusalem, threw the besieging Jews into fear; and they left the siege half-finished and went away. When Varus blamed the Jerusalemites for the war, they said that the gathering of the multitude had happened because of the feast, and that the war was from foreigners. And Varus, having sent [soldiers] throughout the country, searched for the causes of the sedition, and finding some, he punished them; for two thousand were crucified. And about ten thousand others surrendered themselves to Varus as he moved against them; he instead sent their leaders to Caesar; Caesar punished only those who, being relatives of Herod, were campaigning with the insurgents. Ambassadors arrived in Rome from Judea, and when Caesar had established a council, the ambassadors, having brought along those of the Jews in Rome, numbering about eight thousand, requested autonomy. And they turned to an accusation of the lawless acts of Herod; for they did not call him king, but a tyrant. For they said that of the many evils which had often happened to the nation, none was equal to the evils brought upon it by him. Supposing, therefore, that everything would be more moderate under the rule of Herod’s son, they had gladly hailed Archelaus as king. But he, fearing that he might not be considered the son of Herod if he appeared gentler to the Jews, made a slaughter of three thousand men in the Temple, and this even before you, Caesar, had confirmed his rule to him. Saying these things, they requested to be delivered from kingship and such-like authorities, and that they should become an appendage of Syria and be subject to the Roman generals stationed there. Those, therefore, asked for these things, while those around Archelaus, defending themselves, cleared both the kings—I mean the father and the son—of the charges, and blamed the Jews for seditions and revolutions. However, Caesar did not declare Archelaus king, but gave him half of his paternal authority, promising to name him king also if he should rule well; the annual tribute of the region assigned to him was six hundred talents. The other half portion he divided in two and distributed to Antipas and Philip, other sons of Herod; and to Antipas, the assigned region provided a contribution of two hundred talents, while the portion handed over to Philip yielded one hundred talents. To Salome, he gave the things which Herod had left behind and the palace in Ascalon; from the whole, a tribute of sixty talents was brought in for her. And these things were thus settled; but a certain young Jew settled himself into the relatives of Herod, since he was similar in form to Alexander, the son of Herod who had been killed. And taking along a fellow countryman, a wicked man capable of confounding affairs, he called himself Alexander, the son of Herod, as if, when others who had been sent to kill both brothers had been slaughtered, they themselves had been saved and stolen away. And with such words he deceived those who encountered him, and from there, having collected very much money, he hastened to Rome. Caesar did not believe that Herod could be easily deceived, but having brought the pseudo-Alexander into his presence, he was not deceived. For he possessed features similar to Herod’s Alexander, but he had been worn down by manual labor, and his body had grown hard, and was not softened by luxury like that of the other. Caesar therefore questioned him about where Aristobulus might be; and when he said that he had been left in Cyprus, so that if any terrible thing should happen to him, the race of Mariamne might not completely perish, Caesar, having taken the pseudo-Alexander aside, encouraged him to tell the truth for the reward of obtaining his life. He then revealed the scheme to Caesar. And Caesar, seeing him strengthened for manual labor, ordered him to the rowers; the man who was conspiring with him in these matters he put to death. Archelaus, having received the ethnarchy, removed the high priesthood from Joazar, son of Boethus, and substituted his brother Eleazar. And he took to marriage Glaphyra, the daughter of Archelaus, and the wife of Alexander his brother, from whom she also had children, it being forbidden by law to take the wives of brothers who had children from the deceased. And having in a little while cast out Eleazar, he introduced Jesus, the son of See, in his place. And as he was completing his tenth year in office, he was accused before Caesar by his subjects of having handled affairs cruelly and in a totally tyrannical manner.
During his time, Caesar, the autocrat of the Romans, died, having reigned for fifty-seven years, six months, and two days; of which time Antony ruled with him for fourteen years, so that the time of his monarchy was forty-three years, or according to some, forty-four. He lived for seventy-seven years. The monarchy was transferred to Tiberius, the son of Caesar’s wife, Julia. Under him, Valerius Gratus was sent to Judea as governor. He deposed Ananus from the high priesthood and appointed Ishmael, the son of Phabi; not long after, he removed him as well and bestowed the honor of the priesthood upon Eleazar, the son of Ananus, and after a year, he introduced Simon, the son of Camithus, in his place. Joseph, called Caiaphas, was the successor to him, who had served for an equal amount of time. And Gratus, having governed Judea for eleven years and having done these things, returned to Rome; and Pontius Pilate arrived, having taken up the governorship. Herod the tetrarch, who was a friend to Tiberius before his rule, after the monarchy, built a city named after himself in Galilee on the Lake of Gennesaret, calling it Tiberias. Pilate, having moved his army from Caesarea to winter in Jerusalem, brought ensigns into the city by night which bore the images of Caesar, although the law forbids the Jews from having images. When the deed was known, a multitude rushed to Caesarea, requesting the removal of the images, and they held vigils for five days for this cause. When Pilate would not grant it, as though Caesar were being insulted thereby, and the multitude would not cease their requests, on the sixth day he surrounded them with armed soldiers and threatened them with death if they did not cease their clamor. They threw themselves prostrate and said that they would gladly receive death. Pilate, having marveled at their nobility and readiness in observing their laws, immediately sent the images back to Caesarea. Having undertaken the construction of an aqueduct into Jerusalem, he spent sacred funds upon this. Therefore, many gathered and cried out against Pilate, demanding that he cease, and some even used insults against him. He ordered a multitude of soldiers, who were dressed in Jewish garments and carried clubs beneath their garments, to surround the agitators, and thus he commanded the Jews to withdraw. When they began to shout insults, he gave the signal to the soldiers, and they punished the agitators with blows. They, being unarmed and being struck, did not think of giving in; rather, many fell, and the others went away wounded. At this time, our Lord and God Jesus Christ also appeared in Judea, concerning whom Josephus says these words verbatim in the eighteenth book of the *Antiquities*: "At this time there appeared Jesus, a wise man, if indeed it is fitting to call him a man; for he was a doer of wonderful works, a teacher of men who receive the truth with pleasure; and he drew to himself many Jews and many also of the Greek nation. This was the Christ. And when Pilate had condemned him to the cross on the accusation of the leading men among us, those who had loved him at the first did not cease; for he appeared to them on the third day again living, as the divine prophets had told these and ten thousand other wonderful things about him. And even now the tribe of the Christians, named from this one, has not died out." Josephus wrote these things when recording history concerning Christ; but in his discourse to the Greeks, which is inscribed *Against Plato on the Cause of the Universe*—of which the holy John of Damascus has also made mention in the book he composed called *Parallels*—he says these things: "For all, both just and unjust, shall be led before the Word of God. For to him the Father has given the judgment, and he, fulfilling the counsel of the Father, comes as judge, whom we address as Christ. For Minos and Rhadamanthus are not judges according to you, Greeks, but rather him whom God and the Father has glorified; concerning whom we have discoursed more minutely in other places to those seeking the truth. This one, making the just judgment of the Father for each, will prepare the just portion for all according to their works. Standing before whose judgment, all men and angels and demons shall utter one voice, saying thus: 'Just is your judgment.' The recompense of which voice brings the just portion upon both; for to those who have done well, it justly provides eternal enjoyment, and to those who are lovers of base things, it distributes eternal punishment. And for these, the fire remains unquenchable and unending, and a certain fiery worm that does not die nor corrupt the body, but remains, surging from the body with ceaseless pain." And he says other things in addition to these. So, therefore, Josephus has written concerning Christ. Now at that time in Rome there was a certain Paulina, both distinguished by the nobility of her lineage and adorned by the excellence of her character, and she possessed wealth in abundance. Being also graceful in appearance and in the prime of life in which women glory, she was adorned with temperance. She lived with Saturninus, a man who lacked nothing compared to her in any of the virtues. Decius Mundus, being of great rank among the knights of that time, was captivated by love for her and attempted to win her with gifts; for he offered two hundred thousand Attic drachmas for one night of union. She, however, would not endure it. And he became more inflamed with love, so that by abstaining from food he betrothed himself to death. There was a freedwoman of his father’s, called Ide, skilled in all kinds of wickedness.
Seeing this young man in such a state, she emboldened him with her words and fanned the flame with her promises, saying that she would need only fifty thousand to carry out the deed for him. Having received the requested money, since she saw that Paulina could not be captured by wealth—for she knew that she was most warmly devoted to the service of Isis, as this deity was considered a god by the Romans at that time—she approached some of the priests of that false god, and by means of money persuaded them to deceive Paulina by whatever schemes they could, and to have her lie with Mundus. They, charmed by the money, set to the task. The most elderly of them went to Paulina, and speaking with her in private, said that he had come as an emissary from Anubis, the god commanding her to come to him because he was in love with her. To her, the report seemed most desirable, if she were loved by a god; she shared what had been announced with her husband, and he consented, knowing the modesty of his wife. She went, therefore, to the temple; and when it was time for sleep, it was prepared for the woman to sleep inside the temple, and the lamps were extinguished. Mundus, for he had been hidden there, enjoyed her all night long, presenting himself to her as Anubis. Then he departed, and she, going to her husband, recounted the epiphany of Anubis, and exulted in her customary associations, as one deemed worthy of communion with a god. On the third day after the event, Mundus met her and said, "Paulina, I have the two hundred thousand, and I have served you without them. The insults you hurled at Mundus—none of them touched me, having given myself the name of Anubis." Upon hearing these things, the woman, coming to the realization of the brazen act, tore her garment and revealed the plot to her husband; he approached Tiberius. The Emperor, having investigated what had occurred, crucified the priests and Ide, tore down the temple, and had the statue of Isis cast into the river Tiber, while the sentence he passed on Mundus was exile, granting him pardon so that he would not be more severely punished due to the violence of his passion. But while these things had been transacted in Rome, in Samaria a disturbance occurred. For a certain man commanded the Samaritans to assemble with him at Mount Gerizim—this is considered by them to be the most holy of mountains—and to those who arrived he promised to display sacred vessels deposited there by Moses; they gathered and were in arms. Pilate, having sent soldiers, killed some, put others to flight, and executed the leaders of those taken alive. The Samaritans came to Vitellius, who was governing Syria, accusing Pilate of the slaughter of their fellow tribesmen. And Vitellius, having sent Marcellus to take charge of the affairs of the Jews, ordered Pilate to depart for Rome to provide a defense before the Emperor regarding the charges against him. So he departed, having governed Judea for ten years, but before he could reach Rome, Tiberius had already passed away. Vitellius, having reached Jerusalem while the feast of the Passover was being celebrated, was received with magnificence; he remitted the taxes on the crops cultivated by the Jerusalemites, and allowed the high priest's robe to remain in the Temple and to be in the possession of the priests just as before; at that time, it was stored in the Antonia. For one of the high priests, the first, namely Hyrcanus, having built a tower near the Temple, and living in it, had the high priestly robe belonging to him kept there, as high priest. The same was done by those after him as by him. Herod, having refurbished that tower more sumptuously, being a friend of Antony, renamed it in his name; and having found the robe in it, he kept it there. Archelaus also did this, and after him the Romans did the same regarding the robe, which remained there under the seals of the priests and the treasurers. When a feast was impending, it was given back to the high priests by the commander of the garrison, and after one day of the feast, it was deposited there again. Vitellius conceded the clothing to the nation. Having deprived the high priest Joseph, who is also called Caiaphas, of the priesthood, he gave it to Jonathan, the son of Ananus the high priest. By the orders of Tiberius, Vitellius made a treaty with Artabanus, the king of the Parthians, who sent his son Darius with many gifts to be a hostage. The tetrarch Herod, being present at the negotiations, immediately sent a letter to Caesar reporting what had happened. Then, when Vitellius sent a dispatch to Tiberius regarding these things, the latter wrote back to learn everything from Herod. This stirred up anger in Vitellius against Herod; he buried his rage deep, and took revenge on him when Gaius had obtained power. At that time also, Philip, who was the brother of this Herod, died in the twentieth year of the reign of Tiberius, having ruled for thirty-seven years. He was in his manner calm and moderate to his subjects, making his progresses with only a few of his chosen men; and as he went along, if anyone approached him needing help, immediately, with his chair being set down in the place—for those who carried it followed—he would sit, listen, and punish those who did wrong and release those who were accused unjustly. Tiberius added his territory to the province of Syria, for Philip had no children. Herod the tetrarch, traveling to Rome, put up at the house of his brother Philip, whom the daughter of Simon the high priest bore to King Herod. Falling in love with Herodias, his brother’s wife, who was the daughter of Aristobulus, their brother, and the sister of the great Agrippa, he spoke to her about marriage. She accepted and agreed to move in with him if he would cast out the daughter of Aretas. And he sailed for Rome on such conditions;
one might say, the celestial bodies; and that their writings are composed also for the sake of ostentation, showing off the power they possessed in writing, and for this reason placing speeches between the events, and employing discourse in a somewhat digressive or even rhetorical manner. And for some, this ambition even ended in dialogue, so that when they wrote about certain people who held heterodox views and were erring concerning the correct doctrines, they would hold disputes with them as if they were present, and refute their evil opinions, and bring forth refutations from the Holy Scripture, or argue against the Jews, and show them to be willfully evil if they would not accept the mystery of our faith, and make use of prophetic quotations. And again, to set themselves against the Greeks, and to bring the contest of those men into the middle, and to mock the myths told by them, and to bring forth their writings for the refutation of their error. And it is also a way to utter wise maxims and to treat of ethics. B These things, they said, seemed to the majority of those who read these historical writings—not to say to everyone—burdensome and superfluous, because they require more leisure, and because, even if some of those who come to read the histories ever have such leisure, it turns out to be a vain thing for them to labor over these things, since the long narratives about battle lines, and wars, and the arrangement of armies, and the rest of similar things, are wont to escape the memory; and as for the speeches and the dialogues, they turn out to be useless for those who study the narrated events. For what benefit will anyone have, they said, from knowing what the demagogue said to the people? Or what the general said to the soldiers? Or what that emperor said to the envoys, perhaps from the Persians, or others from the Celts, or Scythians, or those from Egypt, or those from the Daci and the Triballi? Or what someone else spoke in a speech to the senate, or to the public gathering? Such things, they said, were what caused the writings of the histories to be published, that is, expanded and leaning toward a more ambitious style. On the other hand, they asserted that other writers labored under the opposite fault, who, while affecting brevity, defrauded the reader of the knowledge of necessary matters: having passed over, or at most having only briefly touched upon, the deeds of outstanding men, which deserve to be celebrated with divine praises, nor declaring what their habits were, what their characters, what their intentions, nor how each of the kings attained the throne, nor who he was before that, nor from what ancestors he was born. They added that some of those writings were published too unadorned, and composed in a harsh style and in plebeian language; whence it happened that learned men abhorred reading them. By saying these things, and by criticizing the writings of the historians in this manner, they have often exhorted me, that when I took books into my hands, I should both pass over those things which on account of their prolixity the memory would not retain, and also those which have no utility: and having reduced the long narration into a compendium, I should edit a brief history, [presenting those deeds and matters more worthy of memory than]
the common and sometimes barbarous expressions; so that, for this reason, those who have been accustomed to elegant language might find them disagreeable. By saying such things, and by thus attacking historical writings, as has been said, they often urged me to take the books into my hands, and, having passed over the many things—of which some, on account of their number, escape the memory, while others result in no utility—to edit a concise history, teaching those who read the work the more timely of the deeds, or those otherwise happening to the persons about whom the writing speaks. They, therefore, urged me to strip for such a struggle, and to set my hand to such a work.
II. But I, while being of a relaxed mind (for I shall speak the truth) and given to leisure, and perceiving that the matter was one of great occupation, and one that demanded an abundance of books, hesitated and shrank from such an undertaking. But they did not cease to goad me until, by the importunity of their urging, they had at last forced me to undertake the work. For if a drop of water, by constantly falling, hollows out the hardness of solid rock, how much more shall speech, striking the ears repeatedly, stir up the sloth of the mind and a relaxed resolve? Then it came to my mind that that labor, and the occupation of writing history, would also bring some utility and even profit to the soul. For the spirits of wickedness are accustomed to breathe more upon a quiet mind, and to stir up the billows of base thoughts and reasonings, one after another, and to overwhelm it by the continuity of their assaults, and either to urge it toward sins (if not in deeds, at least in consents), or meanwhile to stir up great turmoil and disturbance in it. But the mind occupied with certain tasks is usually able to escape the mountainous waves of many evil thoughts and desires. Therefore, both by the exhortations of my friends and by the desire to expel sordid or vain cares, I was led to undertake this work. And so it came to pass that I accepted this task. Should the individual parts of this work be found less than exquisitely perfect, I ask for pardon from the readers. For perhaps, as I now dwell in this retreat, neither will there be available to me the abundance of books necessary for composing history, nor have all the writers recorded the same things about the same matters, but they differ in many—if not in most—points. If I were to desire to examine everything curiously and to recount what this and that person has said about the same matter, for me too would arise a long work about every single thing. Wherefore, it has seemed best to me to pass over the contradictory narrations of the historians, unless something should be of the greatest moment: by omitting which, the very integrity of the history might totter. Now, that the diction is varied, and not everywhere consistent with itself, let no one wonder.
If, however, the character of the discourse changes and is not entirely consistent with itself throughout, let no one wonder, nor let anyone blame the discourse or me, its author. For since I am obliged to collect history like a forage from many books, I must in many places employ the diction and composition of those writers. Yet, wherever I myself may have inserted or added something, the style shall be accommodated to the form of the author I am following, so that the writing might not seem inconsistent with itself.
III. Regarding the preliminary summary of the history III. But indeed, before I approach the subject itself, one must briefly summarize what is to be treated, so that readers may know how many and how very necessary histories they are about to learn. Therefore, this Compendium contains the first eight books of the Bible and what is narrated in them; likewise the books of Kings and the Chronicles, and whatever is found in the Antiquities of the Hebrew Josephus, or is reported by older writers either more copiously or slightly differently than how it was done by them; and the books of Ezra, and the captivities of the Hebrews, of which the first of the ten tribes occurred under the Assyrian Salmanassar, who captured Samaria and the nation by siege, and led them away beyond the Euphrates, and translated certain nations to Samaria who were named Chuthaeans; the other [occurred] under Nebuchadnezzar, with Jerusalem captured, the city deserted, the temple burned, and the whole nation taken into slavery; and [it tells] how, after seventy years, according to the oracles of the prophets, it was permitted by Cyrus, who had overthrown the kingdom of the Assyrians, for the people to return to Jerusalem, and to restore the city, and renovate the temple; who this Cyrus was, and how he overthrew the kingdom of the Assyrians, and who held that kingdom after him, and how and by whom the building of the city was forbidden, and by whom its restoration was again granted. It will also treat of the prophet Daniel, [telling] how he interpreted the dreams of Nebuchadnezzar, and the vision of Balthasar, when that barbarian saw the fingers of a hand writing on the wall, and of certain visions of the prophet: all of which are reviewed with brief narration added. D Likewise of the three youths, and of the miracles performed by God either upon them or through them; of Esther, how she averted the destruction of the Hebrew race; of Judith, who by guile slew Holofernes and delivered his army to destruction; and of Tobias, how he was struck with blindness, and on account of poverty was reduced to extreme destitution, and, on account of his beneficence, by the providence of God recovered his sight and the abundance of riches. Furthermore, the deeds of Alexander the Macedonian are touched upon here, mention of whom had to be made both for other reasons and because, after the first defeat of Darius received at Issus, he journeyed to Jerusalem and held the high priest in the highest honor; [it tells] how he occupied the destroyed Persian kingdom, how long he reigned, how the empire was divided into four kingdoms after his death; what the Jews suffered under Antiochus Epiphanes, one of the posterity of Alexander’s successors, and how those Asamoneans resisted them and delivered their own people from his tyranny; who they were, and how and when...
they assumed leadership over their countrymen, and how they laid claim to the high priesthood, and how they guided that nation, and placed the diadem upon their own heads; and how, when Hyrcanus and Aristobulus, the brothers, were in conflict over the Judean kingdom, Pompeius Magnus, the Roman general then summoned to act as arbitrator between the kinsmen, captured the city of Jerusalem and subjected the nation to the Romans; and how Herod, son of Antipater, subsequently gained the kingdom of the Jews, who he was, whence he originated, and what domestic calamities befell him, and how long his descendants held the kingdom, and in what manner and for what reason governors were sent into Judea; and what Josephus wrote concerning our Savior Jesus Christ, and concerning John the Baptist; for what cause the Jews revolted from the Roman empire, how their nation was warred upon by the Romans, by whom Jerusalem was besieged, and how it was ultimately destroyed, never to be restored.
IV. The History of the Romans and Rome
Since mention has been made of the Romans and of Rome, I have deemed it necessary also to write and set forth from whence the Roman nation originated, from whom it received its beginning, and whom the region of Italy first inhabited; whence Romulus, the founder of Rome, was brought into the light; how his brother Remus was slain, and how he himself later departed from among mortals; how that city was at first ruled by kings, and what customs and laws it employed; how Tarquinius Superbus, having altered the kingdom into a tyranny, was expelled, and how many and what kind of wars Rome endured by reason of his expulsion; how the Roman state was governed first by the aristocracy, then by the people; how supreme power was transferred to consuls and dictators, and soon after to the tribunes of the plebs; what the consulship was in ancient times, what the dictatorship was, what the office of the censors was, what length of time was fixed for each of these magistracies, what the triumph was among them, and from whence that name was derived; and we shall recount what was done during the times of the consuls, though not all things, due to the scarcity of books in which these matters are recorded.
