We shall also tell how at last the Roman government fell under the power of one man, how Gaius Julius Caesar was the first author of this change, although not of the absolute power itself, and how, after he was killed in the senate house by those who defended liberty, Octavius Augustus Caesar, the nephew of the slain Caesar by his sister and his adopted son, avenged his father’s murderers with the aid of Antonius, whom later, *dissension having arisen,
he overcame in a naval battle near Actium. Then, having caught up to him as he fled to Alexandria with Cleopatra, he brought the man to such a state of necessity that he even took his own life. B How great was the slaughter of the Romans in these civil wars—first when Octavius and Antonius pursued Cassius and Brutus and the others who had killed Caesar, and then when they waged war against each other—how Cleopatra, the Queen of Egypt and descendant of the Ptolemies, was taken prisoner, and how she too took her own life, as it is conjectured, by the bite of an asp; how the illustrious victor Octavius thus entered Rome, usurped the empire for himself alone, and transformed the Roman principate entirely into a monarchy; who succeeded him in the empire, as each one received it, how long he reigned, and what end of life he met; who, after the holy apostles, adorned the thrones of the four great Churches—namely, Rome, Alexandria, Antioch, and Jerusalem—and who of these were received into the number and dignity of the martyrs; how the noteworthy rabies of Diocletian and Maximian Herculius against the Christians stood out above the rest, how they abdicated the empire and appointed other Caesars in their place, one of whom was Constantius Chlorus, the father of the great Constantine, to whom the empire of the Gauls and Britain had been assigned; how he, dying, designated his firstborn son, this Constantine equal to the apostles, as his successor in the empire; how he alone became master of affairs, having vanquished the others, when the sign of the cross appeared to him formed by stars in the heavens; how he betook himself to Christ and propagated the faith, having been granted the power to preach the Gospel freely; how he built for himself a city naming it after himself at Byzantium, bestowing the name New Rome upon it, and transferred the empire from the elder Rome to this one; who reigned there after him, what the character of each one was, with what piety, how long each held the empire, and how each departed from life; who were at the head of the Church of Constantinople, and how long each served; which of them held the orthodox opinion and which became heterodox; how each one of those who lived here departed this life; and under which emperors and patriarchs, and with whom, councils were assembled. Thus, the discourse, descending as far as the emperors of our own time, will complete the history, meanwhile making mention of the facts of many hidden matters.
that the celestial powers came into existence before all others by the Creator of all things. But because that Lesser Genesis is not ranked by the divine Fathers among the approved books of Hebrew wisdom, I consider nothing written in it to be certain, nor do I assent to its reasoning.
Then He established the firmament, stretching it out in the midst of the waters, so that some might be held above it and others flow around the earth below. It was called "firmament" because its body is solid, and it does not possess a thin and rare nature like the waters from which it derived its structure, and it was named "heaven." These things happened on the second day. On the third, God ordered the water that was covering the entire face of the earth to be gathered into one place and that the dry land should appear. And the word became deed, and deep hollows formed and received the waters. He named the dry land "earth" and the collections of waters "seas." On this same day, plants and seeds were brought forth from the earth. For it was necessary that the earth, being unadorned, should be decorated with grasses, and myriad sprouts, and flowers, and fruits of every kind, and the delights of trees.
The fourth day saw the creation of the luminaries, the sun and the moon and the remaining stars. With these the Creator decorated the heaven, and He placed into these luminaries the light that was seen on the first day, having marked out for them motions, and risings, and courses, and settings, so that the region of the earth might be illuminated by them and time might be numbered by their periods; and that they might provide to those who observe their settings most uprightly—and not those who observe them with excessive curiosity or, as one might say, those who refine the argument foolishly and believe that all our affairs are swept along with the motion of such stars—a great and unfailing accuracy and utility.
On the fifth day, again, at God’s command, the waters brought forth crawling creatures of living souls, and birds. Some of them dwell in the waters and swim in them; the birds, however, cut through the air, crawling, as it were, through it as they move on high. As to the sixth day, the earth was commanded to bring forth living soul—quadrupeds, and beasts, and crawling things; and everything came forth according to the divine command. Scripture says, furthermore, that the production of the living soul was commanded to the earth, so that in that way it might declare the distinction of the human soul from it; for the soul of other living creatures came into existence from the earth by command, for which reason it is properly considered earthly and mortal, whereas Scripture says elsewhere...
issues from the east, whom Josephus says the Greeks call the Nile. The one which follows this is the Tigris, which the same author affirms is called Diglath, by which word its sharpness or speed with narrowness is signified. The remaining one is the Euphrates; but this word declares either impetus, or flower, or dissipation: and both of these empty into the Erythraean Sea. Furthermore, God presents all living creatures to Adam, so that he might give a name to each kind of them. And God creates woman, having taken one of the ribs of Adam while he was sleeping, and brought her to him. And he recognized her as having been made from himself, and called her woman. For in Hebrew the woman is called Eve, and by that name she had been called. Both of them, therefore, spent a blessed life in paradise, being naked in their simplicity, and in a life devoid of deceit. The serpent, or rather the intelligible dragon who is understood by the serpent, envies them this. And the serpent approaches the woman and counsels her to taste of the tree of knowledge; Josephus says that at that time all animals spoke with one voice. C She is persuaded, and delighting in the eating, she persuades her husband also to partake of the fruit. And they recognized that they were naked, and from fig leaves they made coverings for themselves. And Adam and the woman hid themselves from God on account of their nakedness; and God asked for the cause, and Adam offered the excuse of his nakedness, and shifted the blame onto the woman. She, however, said that she had been deceived by the serpent. God, having first cursed the serpent, deprived him of his feet, and took away his voice, according to Josephus, and ordered him to crawl upon the ground, and declared him an enemy to those whom he had deceived; and having turned to the woman, he renders her subservient to her husband, and punishes her with the pains of childbirth. Then he also imposes a penalty upon Adam: this was the turning back to the earth, from which he was taken, and the eating of bread in the sweat of his face, and the earth bringing forth thorns and thistles for him. And he removes them from paradise, having clothed them in leathern garments—perhaps a sign of the denser and mortal flesh, and a type. From this comes our laborious and painful life, and our liability to vice.
III. They have children, therefore, of whom the first was Cain (the name signifies "acquisition," because they had acquired a man), and the second was Abel (the name denotes "mourning," for there was to be mourning for them on his account). And Abel was a shepherd of a flock, and was intent upon virtue. But Cain tilled the earth, being a most wicked man. And when it seemed good to them to offer the first-fruits of their own toils to God, Abel offered the better of his first-born and his flocks, while Cain offered whatever came to hand from the fruits of the earth. When
Mankind multiplied and inclined toward wickedness; and they had children who were insolent and lawless, through their reliance upon their own strength. The Divine was angered at them and curtailed the longevity of life, limiting the life of humans to one hundred and twenty years. Noah found grace before God. Accordingly, when the continent was turned into sea due to a heavy and long-lasting downpour of rain, the whole human race perished. Noah alone was saved by the providence of God in a wooden ark, which he constructed in a rectangular shape, having a length of three hundred cubits, a width of fifty, and a depth of thirty. Into it he entered, he himself and his sons with him, and his mother, and their wives, having also brought in seeds of every kind, and animals likewise of every species, for the preservation of their kinds—two of the unclean, and seven of each species of the clean. Noah was the tenth numbered from Adam, and one thousand two hundred and forty-two years had passed up to the time of the flood. When the floodgates of heaven were opened for forty days and nights, the water rose fifteen cubits above the summits of the highest mountains. When the rain ceased and the water began to recede after one hundred and fifty days, the ark grounded upon a certain mountain in Armenia in the seventh month. Thereupon Noah opened it and sent out a raven, but it did not return. After some days, he sent out a dove, and it returned. Seven days having passed, he released it again, and it returned carrying an olive branch. From this he knew that the water had subsided from the earth. And Noah went out, and his wife, and his sons with their wives, and the animals that were with them in the ark, and he sacrificed to God. Josephus says that the place is called "Place of Descent" by the Armenians, and that remnants of the ark are shown there. He adds that many of those who have written barbaric histories—such as Berosus the Chaldean, and Hieronymus the Egyptian, who wrote the Phoenician antiquities, and Mnaseas of Damascus—make mention of the flood and the ark. God said that He would no longer bring such a calamity upon the earth, and He gave instructions to use the animals for food, except that they should not eat flesh in blood, as it is the life, and He forbade the killing of men. As a sign that He would not again destroy the earth with rain as was done in the flood, He set the bow in the cloud, understanding the rainbow by the bow. Noah, having lived three hundred and fifty years after the flood, died at the age of nine hundred and fifty years. Josephus says this regarding the multitude of years of the life of that generation: "Let no one, therefore, looking at this life and the scantiness of years, deem false the things recorded about the ancients. For it is not to be wondered at that those who were dear to God, and beings created by God Himself, and who used foods better suited to longevity, lived for such a span of time. Another cause of their longevity was the utility of the things discovered by them, namely astronomy and geometry, which could not have been observed and explained by them with certainty unless they had lived for six hundred years, which is the period in which the Great Year is completed." Such is his account.
No one, therefore, looking at this life and the scarcity of years, should consider false the things recorded about the ancients. For those who were beloved of God, and created by God himself, and who—because they used more suitable foods—lived a life [longer than the current one].
Chetim cultivated the island; this is Cyprus, which the Greeks name after their goddess Cypris, that is, Venus. The children of Cham held Syria and the mountains of Amanus and Libanus; and they occupied whatever of that land faces the sea, as far as the Ocean. From Chus arose the Chusaeans, that is, the Ethiopians. Mesrem was the founder of the Mesraeans (thus the Egyptians are called, and the land of Egypt is named Mesren). Phut inhabited Libya, and named the inhabitants of the region Phutaeans, and the region itself Phutam. Afterwards its name was changed to Libya, from Libys, the son of Mesrem. Chanaan inhabited the land later called Judaea, giving it his own name: the people were called Chanaanaeans. The children of Mesrem held the land from Gaza as far as Egypt. Yet only a part of his portion retained the name Philistim, which the Greeks called Palestine. Sidonius, the firstborn son of Chanaan, founded a city in Phoenicia and called it Sidon. Amathes founded the city of Amatha, which the Macedonians inhabited; Ptolemy, surnamed Epiphanes, changed the name of this city to Epiphaneia. To Sem, son of Noah, five sons were born, who inhabited Asia from the Euphrates as far as the Indian Ocean. He left Elam, the ancestors of the Elymaeans and Persians. Asur founded the city of Ninus and called his people the Assyrians; and Arphaxad was the author of the Chaldaeans. Aram called those who were later named Syrians the Arameans, after himself. Lud begat the Ludi, who were later called Lydians. Now since there were four sons of Aram, one founded Trachonitis and Damascus, which lies between Palestine and Coelesyria; another, the Armenians; another, the Bactrians. The son of Arphaxad was Cainam, and the son of Cainam was Sala. He is the father of Eber. Eber begat Phalec, so named because of the division of the earth; for when the men of that time were divided, a partition of the earth also took place. The Hebrews call the partition *Phalec*, and the nation has its name from its author, Eber. The son of Phalec was Ragab, and Ragab was the father of Seruch. He begat Nachor, Nachor begat Thara, and Thara begat Abraham.
VI. Furthermore, the author of the Hebrew race and the patriarch of his own nation was this great Abraham, by birth a Chaldaean, the tenth from Noah, two hundred and ninety-two years having passed since God had submerged the entire earth with the waves. Being born of impious ancestors, he was the first to recognize the one God, the Maker of the universe, having discovered Him by the evidence of His works, and to whom alone honor is to be rendered.
Since the Chaldeans raised a controversy with him on this account, he migrated from there, at the bidding of the God whom he had recognized, and occupied the land of Canaan. But when famine pressed upon Canaan, he moved to Egypt. And having been admired for his wisdom, he taught arithmetic and the matters concerning astronomy to the Egyptians, who were then unlearned in those arts; for it is said that these things traveled from the Chaldeans to Egypt, and from there to the Greeks (29).
When the Assyrians attacked the Sodomites—with whom lived Lot, the nephew of Abraham and brother of Sarah—and many of the Sodomites had fallen, and the rest were taken captive, Lot among the others was also captured. Upon learning this, Abraham, with three hundred and eighteen of his household servants and three friends, pursued the Assyrians and saved both the Sodomites and Lot. As he returned from the pursuit, the king of the city of Solyma, named Melchisedec (which translated into the Greek dialect signifies "righteous king"), met him. For Solyma was the name then given to the city of Jerusalem; so exceedingly ancient is that city, and in time it has received the rewards of seniority over all others.
God, having approved of Abraham’s virtue, promised him a reward for it. But when he said that every benefit seemed unpleasant to him, as he lacked an heir (for he had not yet received genuine offspring, because Sarah’s womb was obstructed), God promised that He would give him a son, and that from him a great nation would emerge, equal in number to the stars; and He commanded that he and the males under him circumcise their private parts, so that his lineage might not be mixed with other nations; and thus they were circumcised.
Then, when Abraham was now one hundred years old, a son was born to him by Sarah, whom God ordered to be called Isaac. The name signifies laughter, either because a smile occurred to Sarah, when God had announced that she would give birth, because of the hopelessness of the promise, she being at an advanced age and not expecting to bear (for she happened to be ninety years old when she bore Isaac), or because the child became an occasion of laughter for his parents, that is, of joy and gladness. And when this child was born, the father circumcised him on the eighth day. Hence this is how it has become customary for the Jews to be circumcised.
But the Arabs are accustomed to circumcise their boys after the thirteenth year, because Ishmael, their forefather, had been circumcised at that time. For he was thirteen years old when God commanded circumcision to Abraham. However, the name Ishmael, given by God, signifies "God-heard," as God had heard the petition of Abraham and gave him a son. He had sired this son by a maidservant whom Sarah had placed with him. For since she was then incapable of bearing children herself, she asked her husband to lie with her domestic maidservant, an Egyptian by descent, whose name was Agar; and from her was born Ishmael, whom she claimed as her own son. Later, however, after Isaac was born, she banished Agar with her son out of jealousy.
When Isaac had grown up, or, as the Hebrew Josephus records, had become a man (for he says that Isaac was then fifteen years of age), God put Abraham to the test and commanded him to sacrifice his son, and upon a certain mountain to offer up that only-begotten and beloved one. Abraham did not refuse: but taking his son, and...
leading him away to the mountain commanded him, he shares the divine oracle with him and encourages him to bear the consecration bravely, since God has so commanded. He obeys, and willingly offers himself as a victim, saying that it is not right to oppose the will of God and his father. Thus these things are related by Josephus. Then Abraham is commanded to spare his son, and a ram is provided of its own accord in place of the burnt offering. After these things Sarah dies, being one hundred and twenty-seven years old. Abraham takes another wife, named Keturah, from whom six sons are born to him. When Isaac was about forty years old, his father betroths for him Rebecca from Mesopotamia, the daughter of Bethuel, son of Nahor, Abraham’s brother. Afterwards he dies, having lived one hundred and seventy-five years. Having taken Rebecca, Isaac begets twin sons from her, of whom the elder was born with hair covering his body, whence he was also called Esau, on account of the hairiness. The hand of the younger held the heel of his brother, as he slipped out from the mother’s womb, and for this reason he was named Jacob. The name signifies "supplanter" according to the Hebrews.
VII. Having grown old, Isaac was deprived of his sight. And calling Esau (for he had been preferred to the other on account of the birthright), he commands him to hunt, and to prepare a meal for him, so that (he says) having eaten, I might bless you before I die. And Esau went out for the hunt. But Rebecca, loving Jacob more, and calling him, told him what his father had commanded Esau. And she exhorted him to hasten to the flock, and to bring her two tender kids, so that she might prepare dishes from them for the old man, such as she knew he loved, and that having eaten, he might bless him. And he does according to his mother’s commands. And when the dishes were already prepared, she wraps the arms of the boy with the skins of the kids, and gives the meal to take to his father. Isaac, sensing by the voice that it was Jacob who was approaching, calls him very close, and touches his hands. But because God had judged Esau utterly unworthy of the blessing, Isaac thought that his firstborn son stood before him (perhaps deceived by the roughness of the hair), and having eaten, he blessed Jacob. And Esau returned, and brought the meal to his father, and asked for the blessing. He recognized the deception, and having come, said: "Your brother has taken your blessing." And as he lamented and petitioned to be blessed (for he thought there was not even one blessing with him), being entreated, Isaac moved to bless him. But because it did not seem right to the Holy Spirit sounding within him that Esau should receive the blessing—since he was not worthy of it—Isaac’s voice was shaped into a curse instead of a blessing. For these reasons Esau became hostile toward his brother. But he, fearing his brother, by the counsel of his mother departs for Mesopotamia. While going, he is deemed worthy in a vision,
of the celebrated spectacle of the ladder, the ends of which were fixed, the one part on the earth, the other in heaven, and he is initiated into future things by the Superior, and the place where he saw the vision he called Bethel, which signifies "the House of God" in the Greek tongue. Having arrived at Carrhae, he was welcomed by Laban, the brother of his mother Rebecca, and having been hospitably received by him, he is entrusted with the care of his flocks. Now Laban had two daughters, of whom the elder was named Leah, and the younger Rachel. Jacob was seized with love for the younger, as she was more fair of form than her sister, and he approached Laban regarding her. He agreed to betroth Rachel to Jacob if he would serve him for seven years. The lover agrees; time glides by, and the father-in-law prepares the wedding. But at night, he brought the daughter of greater age into the bridal chamber to Jacob. When he recognized the deception in the morning, he remonstrated with his father-in-law about the injury. He, however, promised him Rachel also, after the service of another seven years. Jacob, overcome by love, accepts the condition again; and, when the other seven years had passed, he also obtained Rachel in marriage. Leah already had children from Jacob: she calls the firstborn Reuben, because through the mercy of God he had come to be for her. For she was stung by the husband’s love for her sister, and she hoped that she would be more honored if she should bear children. The second she names Simeon, which signifies that God had hearkened to her prayers. After him, Levi is born, which signifies the confirmation of the union. Judas follows him, a name which sounds of thanksgiving. Rachel, because she had no children, and fearing lest she be less favored, prays her husband to consort with her handmaid; he complies. And from that congress of Jacob with Balla (for that was the name of the handmaid), Dan is born; this word signifies "divine judgment." Again she bears Nephtalim, which is to say "astute," because with this shrewdness she had set herself against the fecundity of her sister. Leah, provoked by emulation, betroths her own handmaiden Zelpha to Jacob, who bore Gad, which could be called "fortuitous." After this, she brought forth another, Asser, whom you may interpret as "prosperous," because Leah was to be held famous and blessed through that birth on account of the multitude of her children. But when Ruben, the son of Leah, had brought mandrake apples, Rachel sought that a part be given to her, with the concession to Leah of that night of the husband’s bedding which was owed to her; for that night had been allotted to Rachel. For this reason, Jacob consorts with Leah, and she bears children: yet two more, Issachar, which name signifies "one born of wages," and Zabulon, which signifies a pledge of benevolence toward her; and a daughter named Dina. There is also born to Rachel a son, Joseph; that name denotes the addition of some future thing. D
VIII. Thus for twenty years did Jacob serve as a shepherd for his father-in-law.
Since he wished to depart, he had not been permitted. Wherefore he fled secretly with his wives, his handmaids, and his children, taking with him his possessions and the cattle which had been assigned to him as his wages for pasturing. Rachel also carried away the idols of her father, not that she worshipped them, but to have them as a safeguard if her father, pursuing them, should catch them. When Laban had caught up to them on the seventh day, he was warned by God in a dream to be reconciled to Jacob. The next day, after words had been exchanged between the two, and even though Jacob had demonstrated that no injury had arisen from himself, Laban nevertheless accused him regarding his ancestral gods. Jacob, knowing nothing of that matter, permitted him to search. Laban searched. But Rachel had hidden the idols underneath the saddle of the camel upon which she was riding, and she sat upon them, saying that she was troubled by the onset of her menstruation. Laban, having the conviction that his daughter would never approach his gods while affected by such a malady, passed her by. So he departed; but Jacob, fearing his brother, sent word to him of his return, having sent gifts on ahead. When night came upon him, he wrestled with a phantom he encountered, and he seemed to prevail. Having perceived that it was an angel of God, he entreated him to tell him what fate he would have. He declared that a victory over a divine angel was to be held as a sign of great blessings, and he commanded him to be called Israel. Some interpret this word as a mind that sees God. Josephus, however, says it signifies one who has resisted a divine angel. Jacob called that place Phanuel, that is, the face of God. And because he had been affected with pain in the hip during the struggle with the angel, he himself abstained from eating it, and all his posterity abhors it as unlawful. His brother Esau, having come to meet him, spoke a greeting and, having accepted it, departed. Jacob arrived at Shechem. Now as the Shechemites were celebrating a festival, his daughter Dinah entered the city in order to see the festivities. Shechem, the son of the king of the city, saw her and raped her by force. Then his father asked Jacob to betroth Dinah to Shechem. He did not consent. Simeon and Levi, brothers of the girl by the same mother, because those in the city were feasting and intoxicated due to the festival, entered the city by night and slaughtered all the males, as well as the king and his son. This is what Josephus relates. But the book of Genesis relates these things more credibly. For it says that when Hamor, the father of Shechem, asked for Dinah as a wife for his son, Simeon and Levi said that if all the males of the city were circumcised, they would betroth their sister to his son; and that having accepted the condition, all were circumcised. And on the third day thereafter, when they were suffering badly from the inflammation of the circumcision, they were slaughtered by them, without Jacob’s knowledge. God commanded him to be of good courage, though he took that crime grievously. Thence Jacob traveled to Bethel, where he had seen the dream, and performed a divine service, and having progressed, he buried Rachel, who died as a result of childbirth, at Ephrata, the boy being named Benjamin [son of pain] due to the mother's grief at his birth. Having traveled to Hebron, he buried his father Isaac.
Isaac dies a little later and is buried with his wife in the ancestral tomb, having lived one hundred and eighty-five years.
IX. Esau was ruling over Idumea, having given the region his own name. For he had been called Edom, because upon returning hungry from a hunt, when he asked his brother for the lentils he had cooked for himself, he was compelled by oath to concede to him his right of primogeniture. From this he was surnamed Edom, on account of the very red color of the lentils. Moreover, "Edom" signifies "red" in Hebrew. Jacob loved Joseph more than his other children, both for the elegance of his face and the adroitness of his mind. And so, both of these attributes and the dreams that he had seen won him the envy of his brothers. The dreams were of this sort: One was that at the time of harvest, while he was reaping with his brothers, he seemed to be binding and arranging sheaves, and his own stood upright, while those of his brothers ran together and bowed down to his. The other was that the sun and the moon and the rest of the stars seemed to him to be bowing down to him. When he related these visions to his father, in the presence of his brothers, he was eager to know what they meant. But he prophesied happiness for the child, and that the time would come when he would be honored and bowed down to by his parents and his brothers. This the brothers of Joseph bore with difficulty, and they looked askance at the youth. While the children of Jacob were pasturing their flocks in Shechem, their father sent Joseph to check on them. When they caught sight of him, they were incited to kill him. But Reuben advised them not to defile their hands with their brother's blood, but to cast him into a pit nearby so that he might die there, and thus the evil deed would be less severe. The young men having agreed to this plan, Reuben took the youth and gently lowered him into the pit. But Judah, having seen Arab merchants, suggested to his brothers that they sell Joseph to them. This having seemed good, they sold him to the merchants for twenty minas, he being seventeen years old. Reuben went to the pit by night to bring out Joseph and save him, undetected by his brothers, but not finding him there and mourning, he accused his brothers. When the matter was explained by them, he ceased his mourning. They soiled the youth’s tunic with the blood of a goat and brought it to their father, saying that they had neither seen Joseph nor knew how he had perished, but that they had found this tunic, bloodstained and torn. Jacob sat mourning for the youth and wearing sackcloth. Joseph was bought from the merchants by Petephres, an Egyptian man, chief of the cooks to King Pharaoh, who treated him honorably. However, the master’s wife, enticed by his form and adroitness into love, made requests to him regarding union, but met with a refusal. Nevertheless, not the less subdued by the heat of her desire, and despairing of persuading him, she prepared violence. But as the youth fled, leaving his garment behind, she sat down, sad and disturbed; and when her husband returned, she accused Joseph, exhibiting the garment which he had left behind while attempting to violate his mistress. Petephres cast Joseph into prison.
into which the cupbearer of Pharaoh, having fallen into the king’s displeasure, had also been cast, and the chief baker; and as Joseph became familiar with them, he was asked to explain their dreams to them. For the cupbearer seemed to see a vine, from which three branches sprouted, and on them large and ripe clusters of grapes hung. He squeezed these into a cup and offered it to Pharaoh, who received it graciously. Joseph interpreted the dream to signify good things for him; for within three days, he would be restored to his former function, and he begged him to remember him when he was in his prosperity. But the chief baker seemed to be carrying three baskets on his head, two of them full of bread, and the third full of meat and various foods; and these were snatched away by birds descending upon them. Joseph told this man too that the three baskets signified three days, and that on the third day he would be hanged and become food for flesh-eating birds. And it happened to both of them just as Joseph had interpreted.
X. After this, God offered Pharaoh two visions in his dreams as well as the explanation of both. He remembered the visions, but had forgotten the explanations. Therefore, he summoned the wise men of Egypt, told them the dreams, and demanded their meaning; but when they hesitated, he became angry. The cupbearer, although he had forgotten before, now finally recalled Joseph and informed the king about him. He was brought in immediately. The king said to him: "Young man, tell me the meaning of the dreams I have seen. I saw seven fat cows coming up from the river, heading into the marsh, and seven others, thin and wasted with hunger, emerged from the marsh and met them, and after devouring the fat ones, the thin ones remained as they were. Again, I saw seven ears of grain growing from one healthy stalk, bent by the weight of the crops; and near them, I seemed to see an equal number of thin ears, ugly in appearance from their withered state, which also consumed the fruitful and beautiful ones." To this Joseph replied: "Although, O king, there are two dreams, there is only one signification for both concerning the future. For both the cows, an animal accustomed to labor in the plow, being devoured by the inferior ones, and the flourishing ears being consumed by the thin ones, portend a famine of as many years for your region, since the very abundant yield of produce of that many years is to be consumed by what follows. But if you store up the former abundance for the years of barrenness, you will remove the sting of that calamity from the Egyptians." Pharaoh, amazed at Joseph's wisdom, both because of his interpretation of the dreams and because of his counsel, said: "You shall be the interpreter and advisor of these affairs, and also the administrator of the plans." And so he conferred his power upon him, such that he used the seal of Pharaoh, wore purple, and rode in a chariot. This happened to Joseph at thirty years of age, whom Pharaoh called Psothomphanech, that is, interpreter of mysteries.
in tears. In the meantime, the king's sons arrived and reported the murder of Amnon. Absalom fled to his maternal grandfather, the king of Gath, and spent three years living there. As time softened the king's anger, Joab devised a plan to reconcile the king with Absalom. He prepared a woman to go to the king, feigning mourning, and to lament that, having had two sons, one had killed the other, and their kinsmen were seeking to slay the survivor for the murder of his brother. She was to beg the king to grant her the preservation of her son. She, therefore, approached the king and lamented these things passionately. The king promised to grant her the life of her son. The woman expressed her gratitude to him, saying, “But so that I may have a pledge of your promise, be reconciled first, O king, to your own son, and do not, because of the murder of the son who died against your will, add another voluntary death.” The king understood that the woman’s words were a pretext and had been devised by Joab, and he permitted him to bring back Absalom. Joab therefore brought him back to Jerusalem, though it was not permitted for him to come into the presence of his father. After these events, the king was reconciled with Absalom; and having called him, he granted him pardon for having asked forgiveness and promised amnesty for his sin. Having acquired horses and chariots and many followers, Absalom went to the palace every day. By speaking to crowds for their own favor, he made many well-disposed toward him. When four years had passed since his return, he pretended that he wished to go to Hebron to offer sacrifices, as if he had made a vow while in exile. The father assented. He departed and sent messengers everywhere; many flocked to him, and among others, Achitophel, his father's advisor. Thus he was hailed as king by all. When David learned of this, he departed Jerusalem with those who were around him; together with them, those six hundred men whom he had used under Saul also set out with him. No other thing increased his grief and fear more than the defection of Achitophel: nevertheless, he invoked God and committed all events to Him. Chusai, who was a friend of David, also, by his counsel, pretended to defect and joined Absalom. During the journey, Siba, the servant of Mephibosheth, met David, driving a yoke of asses carrying provisions; and accusing his master, [he claimed] that he remained in Jerusalem, waiting for the kingdom to pass to him as the multitude would choose him. David was indignant at these things and granted to Siba everything he had previously given to Mephibosheth. Then Semei, a man of Saul's kin, set upon him along the road, and cast stones at the king, and cursed him, calling him a man of blood and a transgressor of the law. When those with David were eager to slay Semei, the king did not allow it, saying, “The Lord told him to do these things; let him be.” And having reached the Jordan, he there refreshed those who were weary with him. V. Now when Absalom arrived at Jerusalem, to Chusai who approached him he said, “Why, being a friend of my father, have you come to me, having abandoned him?” He replied, “I shall be with the one chosen by God, and I shall be with the one whom all the people have chosen.”
The Egyptians, seeing the Israelites flourishing and having forgotten Joseph, devised various hardships against them. They ordered them to channel the Nile into numerous ditches, to surround cities with walls, to raise embankments through which the inundations of the Nile might be checked, and to construct pyramids; and by these means they harassed the Hebrews. They endured these miseries for a long time, until one of the priests announced to Pharaoh that there would be born about that time among the Israelites a boy who, if he were to grow up, would both humble the Egyptians and raise up his own people. Terrified by this, Pharaoh ordered that every male born to the Hebrews be thrown into the river and killed; and that those parents of children who did not do so should be destroyed, root and branch; he also ordered the births of Hebrew women to be watched by Egyptian midwives. While they were in this state, a certain noble man was deeply troubled by this edict (for his wife was pregnant), and after praying to God, he was warned in a dream not to despair: "For the boy," God said, "whom the Egyptians have spoken of, will be, and he will remain unharmed, and he will be raised unknown to those who plot against him; and after having rescued his own people from Egyptian servitude, he will be celebrated with eternal memory. His brother, furthermore, together with his descendants, shall be my priest forever." His wife secretly bore him a male child, whom the parents nourished for three months. But at last, overcome by fear, they committed his safety to God; and having placed the infant in a basket coated with bitumen, they cast it into the river, with Mariamme, the boy's sister, watching what would become of him. Meanwhile, Thermuthis, the daughter of Pharaoh, playing by the riverbank, saw the basket, ordered it to be picked up, and, having seen the boy, grew fond of him because of his beauty and size. Egyptian women were brought in to nurse the child, but he turned away from the Egyptian breast. Then Mariamme, as if she had intervened by chance, said: "Queen, if a Hebrew woman were called, perhaps the infant would accept the breast of one of his own race." She ordered that one be called; the boy's mother was brought, to whom Thermuthis entrusted his nourishment for a wage, and gave him a name derived from the event. For the Egyptians call water *mos*, and *yses* those who are saved from the water. Having therefore joined both these words together, she used that as the infant's name. Moses is counted as the seventh from Abraham. For he was the son of Amram, Caath was the father of Amram, he was of Levi, Levi of Jacob, Jacob the son of Isaac, born of Abraham. When Moses was three years old, both his tallness of stature and the elegance of his form were adorned by a certain grace of the divine: moreover, an intelligence beyond the capacity of his age shone forth in him. Thermuthis, therefore, being childless, adopted him and brought him to her father. He embraced the boy and playfully placed his diadem upon him: which the boy, as the simplicity of that age dictates, threw to the ground.
and trampled upon with his feet. The sacred scribe, seeing this — he who had predicted that someone born of the Hebrews would humble the power of the Egyptians — cried out and said, "This is that boy, by whose destruction you might free the Egyptians from fear." But Thermuthis snatched him away before this could happen, and God rendered Pharaoh more sluggish in carrying out the slaughter.
When Moses reached manhood, the virtue he possessed became evident to the Egyptians. B The Ethiopians, who were neighbors to Egypt, were ravaging their territory. The Egyptians went to war against them, but were defeated; and the Ethiopians, holding them in contempt, began overrunning the whole of Egypt. In accordance with a divine oracle, Pharaoh asked his daughter for Moses, who had by then reached manhood, to be the general for him against the Ethiopians. She gave her consent, but first bound her father by an oath that he would do him no harm. Entrusted with the supreme command of the war, Moses did not lead his army against the enemy by the usual route, but rather through regions impassable due to a multitude of venomous reptiles; and it was there that he demonstrated his cleverness. He constructed baskets, and having filled them with ibises (which Egypt rears in great numbers, and which become tame), he transported them along that route. Now, these ibises are the enemies of such venomous creatures. He used these birds as the vanguard of his army against the venomous beasts; and, as the Ethiopians did not foresee this, he attacked them, defeated them, and captured their cities. Those who were driven into Saba, the royal city of Ethiopia—which Cambyses later named Meroë after his sister—were besieged. Now, the city was difficult to conquer. But Tharbis, the daughter of the ruler of the Ethiopians, fell in love with Moses and sent envoys to him regarding marriage. He agreed, provided she would surrender the city to him. The deed followed the word: Moses celebrated the wedding and led the Egyptians back to their own land. Yet they hated him, even though they had been saved, and stirred up Pharaoh against him. Yielding to his priests, and incensed by the slaying of the Egyptian who had been scourging a Hebrew—as is recorded in the book of Genesis (even though Josephus passed over this)—Pharaoh plotted against the life of Moses. Yet he, although the roads were guarded, fled secretly through the desert and arrived at the city of Midian, where he was entertained by the priest of that city, who, being possessed of a double name, was called Raguel and Jethro. He gave his daughter Zipporah to Moses in marriage and committed his flocks to his care.
XIII. While Moses was pasturing his flock on Mount Sinai, he was granted the vision of the bush, which burned with fire but was not consumed. Then he also heard a voice from there, which forbade him to advance farther, as the place was divine, and which foretold the things that were to come to pass, commanding him to depart into Egypt to deliver his kinsmen from the troubles there, and, when he had led them out of Egypt, to perform sacrifices in that place.
These things were declared to Moses from the fire. But he was in doubt as to how he might persuade his kinsmen, or force Pharaoh to permit their departure. And God promised that He would most easily bring these things to pass, and for the sake of conviction, he was commanded to cast his rod upon the ground, and when he had cast it, it became a dragon. Then, grasping it, it was a rod again. And having been ordered to put his right hand into his bosom, he brought it out white as lime in color, and then it was restored to its former state. And having been commanded to take some of the nearby water and pour it upon the ground, he sees that which was poured out become blood. He was therefore encouraged by these things and commanded to wait no longer. Having therefore taken his wife, and the children born of her, Gershom—which name signifies coming into a foreign land—and Eleazar—the appellation being taken from divine aid—he hastened to Egypt. And when he approached, his brother Aaron met him. Both then arrived before Pharaoh, and having delivered the commands from God, they urged him not to stand against the divine counsel. When he mocked them, they showed him the signs through their deeds, and the rod cast upon the ground became a serpent. And when the magicians of the Egyptians had done the same thing, and had changed their own rods into dragons, Moses cast his rod upon the ground again, and it was changed into a serpent, and devoured the rods of the Egyptians, which appeared to be dragons. Then he took it back, having returned to its own shape. But as Pharaoh hardened his heart, the waters were changed into blood. Then frogs covered the whole land of Egypt. Upon these things, lice sprang up from the ground. Now the other portents the magicians of Pharaoh seemed to perform, but they were unable to counterfeit the lice. They said, therefore, to Pharaoh, that this is the finger of God. But he was still unyielding, and there came the dog-fly, and after these, the destruction of the cattle. Then, when Moses had scattered the soot of the furnaces into the air, ulcers and pustules broke out upon both the cattle and the men; and again hail rained down, and after it came locusts, and after these, darkness covered the Egyptians for three days. Finally, after all these things, the destruction of the firstborn was brought upon them. For as Pharaoh hardened his heart, God commanded Moses to prepare a sacrifice for the fourteenth day of the month, which by the Hebrews is called Nisan, by the Egyptians Pharmuthi, by the Macedonians Xanthicus, and by the Greeks April. When the fourteenth day was come, they all sacrificed, and with the blood of the victims they smeared the lintels of their houses, and having dined, they burned the remainders of the flesh. Whence from then on the Hebrews celebrate a feast at that time, and they sacrifice, calling the feast Pascha. Now Pascha others translate as "passage," but Josephus as "passing over," because on that night God, passing over the Hebrews, afflicted the Egyptians with the destruction of the firstborn. For this reason they met with Pharaoh and begged that he let the Hebrews go. He ordered it. The Hebrews departed, numbering (of military age only) six hundred thousand. They went out in the month of Xanthicus, on the fifteenth day, at the rising of the moon, in the years. Varia lectiones et notae: (56) Παρά "Έλλησιν 'Απρίλιος. Imo apud Latinos, seu ut habet S. Maximus in Computo Ecclesiastico parte 1, cap. 15, κατὰ Ῥωμαίους. Sed hic ut et alibi non semel 'Έλληνας vocat Zonaras, quos alii Romanos; contra quam Eunapius in Chrysanthio p. 89.
four hundred and thirty after Abraham had arrived in Canaan, and two hundred and fifteen years after the migration of Jacob into Egypt, Moses being then eighty years of age, whom Aaron exceeded by three years. They also carried with them the bones of Joseph, as he had commanded. Thus they set out.
XIV. The Egyptians, acting out of repentance, seized their arms and pursued them with six hundred chariots, fifty thousand cavalry, and two hundred thousand infantry. The Hebrews, reduced to despair, were in great distress and were on the point of stoning Moses. He encouraged them to follow him and led them to the sea. Standing on the shore, having invoked God, he struck the sea with his rod. The sea was parted, so that the dry land appeared to the Hebrews, and they crossed over in haste. The Egyptians, seeing this, placed their cavalry in the vanguard and pursued them. But when the Hebrews had already reached the opposite shore safely, the sea, upon being struck again by Moses with his rod, rushed upon the Egyptians, and its waves closing in, the sea overwhelmed them. Thus all the Egyptians perished in the waters, while the Hebrews joyfully celebrated God with hymns. Moses also composed an ode to God in hexameter verse, as Josephus states. The Hebrews took the arms of the Egyptians, which had been cast ashore along with their bodies by the wind, and armed themselves with them. Moses then led them to Mount Sinai, to offer sacrifices there as he had been commanded.
While they were being led there, they suffered from a scarcity of food and a lack of water. Arriving in such a state at a place where there was a well, the water of which was undrinkable because of its bitterness, the book of Exodus calls this place Marah, but Josephus records that it was called Mar by the Hebrews, for "Mar" in their language signifies bitterness. They murmured against their leader, but he, having first prayed to God, took a piece of wood and threw it into the well, and the water became sweet. Josephus does not seem to believe that the sweetening of the water was a miracle but conjectures that it happened naturally. For he says that Moses, having cast the wood into the water, ordered them to draw the well dry, and while they were intent upon that work, the water, stirred and purified by continuous agitation, finally became drinkable. He also relieved the scarcity of food by praying to God, by means of quails, an immense multitude of which was brought into the camp of the Hebrews and taken, and they ate, satisfying their hunger. Then God rained manna upon them, in sweetness equal to honey, and in size similar to a coriander seed. It was called Manna by them, because they were in doubt as to what the thing was; for "Man" in that language is also a note of interrogation. Thus Josephus records these matters. Now everyone had been ordered to gather an Assaron.
the assaron is a measure); but if anyone gathered more, by the next day it was uneatable, corrupted by worms and bitterness. This was so contrived, so that where the robust had gathered much, it would not be lacking for the weak. They used this food for forty years, for as long as they were in the desert. Then they arrived at a waterless place, called Raphidin, and being thirsty, they were stirred up against Moses. He, having prayed to God, is commanded to strike the rock with his staff, and he strikes; instantly a great and very clear water gushed forth. Then the Amalekites proceeded against the Hebrews, having called their neighbors as allies. Moses, having separated the fighting men of the people from the others, and having appointed Jesus the son of Nave as their leader, with a part of the army left behind for the protection of the water, he himself withdrew toward the mountain—and he ordered Jesus to trust in God and to lead the army out against the adversaries. Already the battle was joined, and as long as Moses held his hands aloft, the Hebrews were dealing grievous blows to the Amalekites. But whenever Moses would let his hands down, exhausted by the extension of them, it would happen that the Hebrews were being pressed hard. Therefore, having stationed his brother Aaron on one side, and Or, the husband of his sister Mariam, on the other, he ordered them to support his hands; and thus the Hebrews, conquering the Amalekites, would have destroyed them all, had not night checked the battle; and of the Hebrews, not even one had died. XV. From there they moved forward, and in the third month after the departure from Egypt, they reached Mount Sinai. There Moses performed a divine rite, and was approached by his father-in-law, Raguel, who, when he saw his son-in-law overwhelmed with the business of all those coming to him, advised that he delegate the province of judicial decisions to others, while he himself, having taken up the care of more important matters, should consult for the safety of the people and appoint prefects who would preside over ten thousands, others over thousands, and five hundreds, and over these, hundred-rulers and fifty-rulers, and over thirty, twenty, and ten, who would manage these affairs. Moses, having followed Raguel’s advice, acts according to his suggestion. Then, having commanded the people to fix their camp near Mount Sinai, he himself ascended the mountain. The people fulfilled his commands, abstaining from other unclean acts, and from the sexual union of wives for three days. On the third day at dawn, a cloud surrounded the camp of the Hebrews, and there were terrible flashes of lightning and a vehemence of winds, which terrified the Hebrews remaining in their tents. But Moses appears full of courage, and having been seen, he removes their fear, and announces to them what God had commanded, and what ordinances they were to keep; and he brings the people forward along with their wives and children, so that they might hear God. And they heard a voice coming from on high. Then again he ascends the mountain; and as he stayed there a long time, the people, flowing toward Aaron, said: "Make us gods: for what has happened to Moses, we do not know." Aaron, compelled by force, commands them to bring the gold earrings and ornaments of their wives and daughters. They bring them, and from them they cast a golden calf and offer sacrifice to it. Moses, descending from the mountain, and
carrying in his hands the two tablets of the testimony, stone tablets inscribed on both sides, as he drew near to the camp, sees the calf, and in his anger shatters the tablets; he takes the calf, burns it, grinds it to powder, and casting it into water, makes the people drink it. He commands the sons of Levi, and they go through the camp and kill many, so that the fallen are counted at about three thousand. Moses returned to the mountain, saying: Lord, thy people hath sinned; now if thou forgivest them their sin, forgive; but if not, blot me also out of thy book. And the Lord said to him: Go down, lead this people into the place of which I spoke to thee. And the Lord showed Moses, who asked to see Him, His back parts. And he descended to the people again, carrying two tablets containing the ten commandments inscribed; and the appearance of his face was glorified, and the people could not look steadfastly at him, so he put a veil over his face, and thus spoke with them. And he announced to them what God had commanded concerning sacrifices and purifications, and concerning animals both unclean and clean, so that they should abstain from the former and be nourished by the latter; concerning illicit unions, and whom the priests may take in marriage, and what kind of women the high priests may take; and concerning the Sabbath, and the seventh year, and the fiftieth, which they call the Jubilee. And he said that God commanded a tent to be built into which He would descend, and which would be transferred with them as they moved; and He showed him the dimensions of the tent, and the shape according to which it should be constructed. And the people, rejoicing at these things, brought gold and silver, and precious stones and bronze, and wood, and goats' hair, and rams' skins, and linen, and wool. He puts in charge of the construction of the tent the son of his sister C Mary, Bezalel, and Eliphaz.
XVI. The tabernacle being now finished, Moses erects it. He also crafted the Ark from incorruptible wood, gilded all around; and in it he placed the two tablets which contained the decalogue. The first affirms that there is one God, and that Him alone must one worship. The second, that no image of any living creature is to be made and adored. The third, by the name of God one must not swear for any vain thing. The fourth, the seventh day is to be observed, and one must abstain from all work on it. The fifth, parents are to be honored. The sixth, one must abstain from murder. The seventh, not to commit adultery. The eighth, not to steal. The ninth, one must not bear false witness. The tenth, nothing of another's is to be coveted. They place this Ark into the inner sanctuary, and in the temple they set a table, and upon it twelve unleavened loaves, and a golden lampstand, and an incense-altar, wooden within, but externally covered with gold. And before the tent he set up a wooden altar, overlaid with iron plates, over against which phials and mixing bowls with censers were placed, and other vessels which were prepared for sacrifices,
Elias then meets Abdias, and bids him announce him to the king. But he, saying that Achab had been seeking him most diligently, so that he might kill him if he found him, advised him to hide; and he himself said that he had hidden and fed a hundred prophets, and thus saved them, all the others having been slaughtered by Jezebel. He swore, however, that on that very day he would be seen by Achab. Abdias went, therefore, and announced Elias to the king. And he, seeing the prophet, asked if this was he who was troubling Israel? But Elias said, "It is rather you and your father's house, who trouble the people, having forsaken the Lord and reverencing false gods. But gather together into Carmel the people, and your own prophets, and those of your wife." When all had gathered, Elias said, "I alone am left a prophet of the Lord, whereas the false prophets are very many. Give me, therefore, a bull, and another to them all; and having sacrificed the bulls, let us place the offerings upon wood, but let us not apply fire; and let each call upon his own God, and he by whom fire shall spontaneously flash forth, and consume the wood and the offerings, let him be judged the true God." The multitude praised his words, and the priests of shame acted accordingly, and having sacrificed the calf, and having placed it on the wood without fire, they invoked Baal from early morning until noon. But the prophet, mocking them, advised them to shout loudly, lest perhaps their god was asleep or was occupied with other business. And when, though they urgently pleaded, they achieved nothing, the prophet, taking twelve stones according to the number of the tribes, built an altar, dug a trench, arranged the wood on the altar, placed the victims, and ordered water to be poured onto the altar so that the trench was also filled. Having finished these things, he invoked the Numine, and fire was brought down from heaven in the sight of all, and it consumed the victims and the stones together with the water and the earth. Struck with amazement at this wonder, the people fell prostrate on the ground, confessing that there is one true and great God; and having seized the false prophets, they killed them at the prophet's command. He then said to the king that within a short time God would send rain. And there occurred, according to his prediction, a heavy downpour. Jezebel having threatened to kill Elias, the prophet, terrified, fled, and arriving in the desert, in despair prayed to God that he might die. Having fallen asleep, he was awakened by someone who bade him arise and eat. He saw there also bread made of coarse grain and water. And having eaten, he slept again; and again the angel commanded him to eat, as a long journey lay before him. And again having eaten and being strengthened by that food, he traveled for forty days. And having arrived at Mount Choreb, he entered a cave, and heard a voice: "Why have you come here?" He said, "Because, acting with zeal, I killed the prophets of Jezebel, and she seeks to kill me." And He commanded him again, saying, "Go out tomorrow, and stand before the Lord." And he did so, and perceives a wind and a commotion, and sees a burning fire. Then, when a gentle breeze arose, he hears a voice from there, commanding him to return, and to anoint Hazael as king of Syria, and Jehu as king of Israel, and Elisha in his own place as prophet, who would be the ones to destroy the impious. Elias returned, as he had been commanded, and found Elisha plowing; and upon him he threw his mantle, and he, leaving his oxen, follows him, having risen to the office of prophecy.
'" When Micah had said these things, Ahab said to Jehoshaphat, "Did I not tell you that he always prophesies evil against me?" And Zedekiah, one of the false prophets, struck Micah on the cheek. But he said to him, "In a little while you shall go from room to room hiding yourself." And King Ahab commanded that he be taken into custody and guarded until he himself should return. And Micah said, "If you return in peace, the Lord has not spoken by me." Long after the kings had set out, the king of Syria drew up forces against them, having instructed his soldiers to fight with no one else, but with Ahab alone; and he exchanged his royal robes for common attire. When the Syrian commanders saw Jehoshaphat decked out in royal fashion, they thought he was Ahab and surrounded him. But when they realized he was not the king of Israel, they fell back. Someone, however, drew his bow and struck Ahab. Knowing that he was mortally wounded, he ordered the charioteer to take the chariot out of the battle; and toward the setting of the sun, he died. The Syrians, knowing that Ahab was dead, departed to their own lands. The king’s corpse, brought to Samaria, was buried. The chariot, stained with the king's blood, was washed away in the pool of Samaria, and the dogs licked up the blood, and the harlots bathed in the pool, as Elijah had foretold. Thus Ahab also died, where Micah had predicted, not escaping the destruction, although it had been foretold to him, having reigned for twenty-two years. His son Ahaziah received the kingdom after him. XV. Jehoshaphat, the king of Jerusalem, returning home from an expedition, is rebuked by the prophet Jehu, for having brought aid to an impious man, and because he was preserved by God, even though He was offended by that deed. The king gives thanks: and after traversing his entire dominion, he commanded the laws to be observed and, having appointed judges everywhere, ordered them to judge righteously. But when the Moabites and Ammonites, who had even taken the Arabs along, attacked Judah, he implored divine help. A certain prophet said that God would fight for him, and commanded him to lead out the army, but not to engage the enemies, but to stand and await divine assistance. In the morning the king went out with the army, commanded them to stand firm, and that the priests should stand before the army with trumpets, and the Levites and singers should sing a hymn of thanksgiving to God. Then the enemies, moved against one another, killed each other, so that not one survived. Jehoshaphat allowed the army to plunder the enemy camp. From that time on, the barbarians, fearing him as one who had God as his ally, remained at peace. Ahaziah, however, the king of the Israelites, was an imitator of his parents' wickedness. In the second year of his reign, the Moabites withheld the tribute which they had been accustomed to pay to his father. Becoming ill, he sent to the Accaronite god, named Musca, to inquire whether he would recover. Elijah met the messengers on the road and ordered them to return home, and to say to the one who sent them that, because he had sent to the god of the barbarians, as if there were no God in Israel, he would not rise from his bed. They returned and reported what they had heard. Ahaziah asked who it was who had said these things. When they said they did not know, he demanded that they describe the appearance of that man. When they said he was hairy and girded with a leather belt, he understood that it was Elijah. And having sent a captain with fifty men under him, he ordered the prophet to be brought. And the one sent, having found him on the top of the mountain, ordered him to go to the king.
And when he had come to the prophet, he bowed down and worshipped him, and begged off the punishment from him, saying that he had come unwillingly, forced by the king's command, and he besought him to go to the king. But Elias, an angel having told him to go and not to be afraid, came down from the mountain; and having come, he said to the king: "Since you have sent to a foreign god, seeking if you shall recover, know that you shall not rise from your sickness, but you shall die." Ochozias therefore died according to the prophecy of the prophet, leaving the kingdom to his brother Joram, who indeed broke in pieces the pillars of Baal which his father Achab had set up; but in other respects he was impious and wicked, and like to his parents. Being about to be taken up, Elias went with Eliseus. And having come to the Jordan, he struck the water with his mantle, and the water was divided, and both crossed over on dry land. Then, "Ask," he said to Eliseus, "what I shall do for you?" And he asked that a double portion of the spirit that was in him should come upon him. But Elias said that the request was a heavy one; "Yet, if you see me being taken up, it shall be so for you." And while they were still speaking, a chariot of fire with horses like it stood by, and snatched up Elias. And as he was being carried up, he threw his mantle to Eliseus. And he, having taken it, and no longer seeing Elias, turned back; and having come to the Jordan, he made a trial whether he had received the grace as he asked, and struck the water with the mantle. But it was not divided. And he said, "Where is the God of Elias?" And he struck again; and the water parted, and he crossed over. And the fifty sons of the prophets, seeing him, said: "The spirit of Elias has rested upon Eliseus." And they followed him, and sought to go, intending to search if the Spirit of the Lord had taken up Elias and cast him upon one of the mountains or the hills. But Eliseus did not permit them. But when they insisted, he yielded. And fifty men went, and they searched for three days, and did not find him. Now Eliseus was in Jericho. And the men of the city came to him, begging that the waters of that place, which were evil and caused infertility, might be changed by him. And he asked that salt be brought to him in a new vessel; and when it had been brought, having invoked the name of God, he cast the salt into the spring of the waters, and the waters were changed from bitterness to sweetness, and nothing harmful remained in them. As the prophet Eliseus was going somewhere, small boys mocked him, crying out: "Go up, baldhead." And being pained at this, he cursed them. And bears came out of the mountain, and killed forty-two of the boys. And Joram the son of Achab, being about to march against the Moabites, sought help from Josaphat, king of Judea. He also promised to the Idumean king, who obeyed him, that he would join him as an ally. So the three kings, having entered into a pact, went for seven days through the desert. But the armies being destitute of water, they approached Eliseus and prayed that he would preserve both them and the soldiers. But he said to Joram: "Go to the prophets of your father and your mother," and he swore that if it were not for Josaphat, he would not have answered him even a word. Then he ordered someone to be called who would play the psaltery. As he played, Eliseus, possessed by the spirit of the Lord, ordered many pits to be dug by the torrent; for they would see the torrent full, neither with wind blowing, nor with rain falling, and they would drink to satiety, and would prevail over their enemies. These things having been predicted, the next morning the bed of the torrent was filled, so that it overflowed and all the surface of that land was flooded. As the sun rose,
